# Buddhism

"Buddhadharma" redirects here. For those who practise Buddhism, see [Buddhists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhists).



The [Kamakura Daibutsu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamakura_Daibutsu), a 13th-century bronze statue of the Buddha [Amitābha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amit%C4%81bha) in [Kanagawa Prefecture](61757.md), Japan

**Buddhism**,[^cite_note-4] also known as **Buddhadharma** and **Dharmavinaya** (transl. "doctrines and disciplines"), is an [Indian religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_religion) and [philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_philosophy)[^cite_note-8] based on [teachings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-sectarian_Buddhism) attributed to [the Buddha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Buddha), a *[śramaṇa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Arama%E1%B9%87a)* (wandering ascetic) and religious teacher who lived in the 6th or 5th century [BCE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Before_the_Common_Era).[^cite_note-9] It is the [world's fourth-largest religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_religious_groups),[^cite_note-10][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELopez2001239-11] with an estimated 320 million to 540 million followers, representing approximately 4.1%–6.7% of the global population. It originated in the eastern [Gangetic plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gangetic_plain) as a *śramaṇa* movement in the 5th century BCE, and gradually spread throughout much of Asia via the [Silk Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silk_Road). Buddhism has since played a significant role in Asian culture and [spirituality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spirituality), eventually [spreading to the West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_the_West) in the 19th century.

According to tradition, the Buddha taught a path of [cultivation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhavana) that leads to [awakening](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enlightenment_in_Buddhism) and [liberation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moksha) from *[dukkha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Du%E1%B8%A5kha)* (often translated as 'suffering';[^cite_note-pain-12] 'unsatisfactoriness') by attaining [nirvana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nirvana_\(Buddhism\)), the 'blowing out' (extinguishing) of the passions. He regarded this path as a [Middle Way](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Way) between extreme asceticism and sensory indulgence,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201323,_81-13][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeown199624,_59-14][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBronkhorst2011233–237-15][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchuhmacherWoener1991143-16] and also between the extremes of [eternalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sassatavada) and [nihilism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nihilism).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201372-17][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBuswellLopez200349,_''antagrahadrsti''-18] Teaching that *dukkha* arises alongside [attachment or clinging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Up%C4%81d%C4%81na), the Buddha advised [meditation practices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_meditation) and [ethical precepts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_ethics) rooted in [non-harming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahimsa). Widely observed teachings include the [Four Noble Truths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Noble_Truths), the [Noble Eightfold Path](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noble_Eightfold_Path), and the doctrines of [dependent origination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dependent_origination), [karma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karma_in_Buddhism), and the [three marks of existence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_marks_of_existence). Other commonly observed elements include the [Triple Gem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triple_Gem), the taking of [monastic vows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_monasticism), and the cultivation of perfections (*[pāramitā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C4%81ramit%C4%81)*).[^cite_note-19]

The [Buddhist canon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tripi%E1%B9%ADaka) is vast, with [philosophical traditions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophical_tradition) and textual collections in different languages (such as [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit), [Pali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pali), [Tibetan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibeto-Burman_languages), and Chinese).[^cite_note-20] [Buddhist schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schools_of_Buddhism) vary in their interpretation of the [paths to liberation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_paths_to_liberation) (*_mārga_*, "path") as well as the relative importance assigned to various [Buddhist texts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_texts), and their specific teachings and practices.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams1989275ff-21][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinsonJohnson1997xx-22] Two major extant branches of Buddhism are generally recognised by scholars: [Theravāda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theravada) (lit. 'School of the Elders') and [Mahāyāna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahayana) (lit. 'Great Vehicle'). The Theravada tradition emphasises the attainment of *[nirvāṇa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nirvana_\(Buddhism\))* (lit. 'extinguishing') as a means of transcending the individual self and ending the cycle of death and rebirth (*[saṃsāra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sa%E1%B9%83s%C4%81ra_\(Buddhism\))*),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGethin199827–28,_73–74-23][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201399-24][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPowers2007392–393,_415-25] while the Mahayana tradition emphasises the [Bodhisattva ideal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bodhisattva), in which one works for the liberation of all sentient beings. Additionally, [Vajrayāna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vajray%C4%81na) (lit. 'Indestructible Vehicle'), a body of teachings incorporating esoteric [tantric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tantra) techniques, may be viewed as a separate branch or as a tradition within Mahāyāna.[^cite_note-White_2000_21-26]

The Mahāyāna branch, which includes the traditions of [Tiantai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiantai), [Chan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chan_Buddhism), [Pure Land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pure_Land_Buddhism), [Zen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zen), [Nichiren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nichiren_Buddhism) and [Tendai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tendai), is the largest branch of Buddhism, being predominantly practised in Nepal, [Bhutan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhutan), China, Malaysia, Vietnam, Taiwan, [Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korea), and Japan. The second-largest branch, Theravada, is followed mainly in [Myanmar](19457.md), Thailand, [Laos](17752.md), Cambodia, and Sri Lanka. [Tibetan Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibetan_Buddhism), a form of *Vajrayāna*, is practised in the [Himalayan states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Himalayan_states) as well as in [Mongolia](19271.md)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPowers200726–27-27] and [Russian Kalmykia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalmykia) and [Tuva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuva).[^cite_note-28] Japanese [Shingon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shingon) also preserves the Vajrayana tradition as transmitted to [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tangmi). Historically, until the early [2nd millennium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2nd_millennium), Buddhism was widely practised in [the Indian subcontinent before declining there](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decline_of_Buddhism_in_the_Indian_subcontinent);[^cite_note-29][^cite_note-30][^cite_note-Keown2004p208-31] it also had a foothold to some extent elsewhere in Asia, namely Afghanistan, Turkmenistan, [Uzbekistan](31853.md), and Tajikistan.[^cite_note-32]

## Etymology

The terms *Buddhadharma* and *Bauddhadharma* come from [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit): बुद्ध धर्म and बौद्ध धर्म respectively ("doctrine of the Enlightened One" and "doctrine of Buddhists"). The term *Dharmavinaya* comes from Sanskrit: धर्मविनय, literally meaning "doctrines \[and] disciplines".[^cite_note-33]

[The Buddha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Buddha) ("the Awakened One") was a [Śramaṇa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Arama%E1%B9%87a) who lived in [South Asia](21566765.md) c. 6th or 5th century BCE.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGethin19987–8-34][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBronkhorst2013ix–xi-35] Followers of Buddhism, called *Buddhists* in English, referred to themselves as *Sakyan*-s or *Sakyabhiksu* in ancient India.[^cite_note-36][^cite_note-37] Buddhist scholar Donald S. Lopez asserts they also used the term *Bauddha*,[^cite_note-38] although the scholar Richard Cohen asserts that that term was used only by outsiders to describe Buddhists.[^cite_note-39]

## The Buddha

Main article: [The Buddha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Buddha)



[The Buddha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Buddha), [Tapa Shotor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tapa_Shotor) monastery in [Hadda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadda,_Afghanistan), Afghanistan, 2nd century CE



[Maya Devi Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maya_Devi_Temple,_Lumbini) marking the Buddha's birthplace in [Lumbini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lumbini), Nepal

Details of the Buddha's life are mentioned in many [Early Buddhist Texts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Buddhist_Texts), but these accounts are inconsistent. His social background and life details are difficult to substantiate, and the precise dates are uncertain, although the 5th century BCE seems to be the best estimate.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGethin199813–14-40][^cite_note-48]



Ancient kingdoms and cities of South Asia and Central Asia during the time of the Buddha (c. 500 BCE)—modern-day India, Nepal, Pakistan, Bangladesh and Afghanistan

Early texts have the Buddha's family name as "Gautama" (Pali: Gotama), while some texts present Siddhartha as his surname. He was born in [Lumbini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lumbini), (present-day Nepal) and grew up in [Kapilavastu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kapilavastu_\(ancient_city\)),[^cite_note-49] a town in the [Ganges Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Gangetic_Plain), near the modern Nepal–India border, and he spent his life in what is now modern [Bihar](442482.md)[^cite_note-51] and [Uttar Pradesh](231623.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich198849-50][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGethin199813–14-40] Some [hagiographic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagiography) legends state that his father was a king named [Suddhodana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Auddhodana) and his mother was [Queen Maya.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maya_\(mother_of_the_Buddha\))[^cite_note-Thomas2013p16-52] Scholars such as [Richard Gombrich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Gombrich) consider this claim dubious because a combination of evidence suggests he was born in the [Shakya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shakya) community, which was governed by a [small oligarchy or republic-like council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ga%E1%B9%87asa%E1%B9%85gha) where there were no ranks but where seniority mattered instead.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich198849–50-53] Some stories about the Buddha, his life, his teachings, and claims about the society he grew up in may have been invented and interpolated at a later time into the Buddhist texts.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich198818–19,_50–51-54][^cite_note-55]

Various aspects of the Buddha's background are contested in modern scholarship. For instance, Buddhist texts assert that the Buddha described himself as a [kshatriya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kshatriya) (warrior class), but Gombrich writes that little is known about his father and there is no proof that his father even knew the term *kshatriya*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich198850-56] ([Mahavira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahavira), whose teachings helped establish [Jainism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jainism), is also claimed by his early followers to have been a kshatriya.)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich198850–51-57]

According to early texts such as the Pali *Ariyapariyesanā-sutta* ("The discourse on the noble quest", [MN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majjhima_Nik%C4%81ya) 26) and its Chinese parallel at [MĀ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madhyama_Agama) 204, Gautama was moved by the suffering (*[dukkha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dukkha)*) of life and death, and its [endless repetition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samsara) due to [rebirth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebirth_\(Buddhism\)).[^cite_note-58] He therefore set out on a quest to find liberation from suffering (also known as "[nirvana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nirvana_\(Buddhism\))").[^cite_note-59] Early texts and biographies state that Gautama first studied under two meditation teachers, [Āḷāra Kālāma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%80%E1%B8%B7%C4%81ra_K%C4%81l%C4%81ma) (Sanskrit: Arada Kalama) and [Uddaka Ramaputta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uddaka_R%C4%81maputta) (Sanskrit: Udraka Ramaputra), learning meditation and philosophy, particularly the meditative attainment of "the sphere of nothingness" from the former and "the sphere of neither perception nor non-perception" from the latter.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWynne20078–23-60][^cite_note-61][^cite_note-64]

Finding these teachings insufficient to attain his goal, he turned to the practice of severe [asceticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asceticism), which included a strict fasting regime and various forms of [breath control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pranayama).[^cite_note-Analayo_2011_p._236-65] This too did not lead to his goal, and he then turned to the meditative practice of *[dhyana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhy%C4%81na_in_Buddhism)*. He sat in [meditation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meditation) under a *[Ficus religiosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ficus_religiosa)* tree now called the [Bodhi Tree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bodhi_Tree) – in the town of [Bodh Gaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bodh_Gaya) and attained "Awakening" ([Bodhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enlightenment_in_Buddhism)).[^cite_note-66]

According to various early texts, such as the *Mahāsaccaka-sutta,* and the *[Samaññaphala Sutta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sama%C3%B1%C3%B1aphala_Sutta),* upon awakening the Buddha gained insight into the workings of karma and his former lives, as well as achieving the ending of the mental defilements (*[asavas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asava)*), the ending of suffering, and the end of rebirth in [saṃsāra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sa%E1%B9%83s%C4%81ra).[^cite_note-Analayo_2011_p._236-65] This event also confirmed for him the [Middle Way](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Way) as the correct path of spiritual practice to end suffering.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBronkhorst2011233–237-15][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchuhmacherWoener1991143-16] As an [awakened Buddha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhahood#Samyaksambuddha), he attracted followers and founded a *[Sangha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sangha_\(Buddhism\))* (monastic order).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich198849–51-67] He spent the rest of his life teaching the [Dharma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharma) he had discovered, and then died, achieving "[final nirvana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parinirvana)", at the age of 80 in [Kushinagar](33686220.md), India.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeown2003267-68][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeownPrebish2010105–106-43]

The Buddha's teachings were propagated by his followers. In the last centuries of the 1st millennium BCE, these teachings developed into various [Buddhist schools of thought](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schools_of_Buddhism), each with its own [collection of texts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tripi%E1%B9%ADaka) containing different interpretations and of the Buddha's authentic teachings.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGethin199854–55-69][^cite_note-70][^cite_note-britannicatipitaka-71] Over time, these evolved into many traditions, of which the more well-known and widespread in the modern era are [Theravada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theravada), [Mahayana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahayana) and [Vajrayana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vajrayana) Buddhism.[^cite_note-72][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGethin19981–5-73]

## Worldview

Main article: [Glossary of Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_Buddhism)

The term "Buddhism" is an occidental neologism, commonly (and "rather roughly" according to [Donald S. Lopez Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_S._Lopez_Jr.)) used as a translation for the Dharma of the [Buddha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gautama_Buddha), *fójiào* in Chinese, *bukkyō* in Japanese, *nang pa sangs rgyas pa'i chos* in Tibetan, *buddhadharma* in Sanskrit, *buddhaśāsana* in Pali.[^cite_note-74]

### Four Noble Truths – *dukkha* and its ending

Main articles: [Dukkha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dukkha) and [Four Noble Truths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Noble_Truths)

See also: [Prapanca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prapanca)

color manuscript illustration of Buddha teaching the Four Noble Truths, Nalanda, Bihar, India

The Buddha teaching the Four Noble Truths. [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit) manuscript. [Nalanda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nalanda), Bihar, India

The Four Noble Truths, or the truths of the [Noble Ones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arhat),[^cite_note-75] express the basic orientation of Buddhism: everything is impermanent, yet we [crave and cling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Up%C4%81d%C4%81na) to [impermanent states and things](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sa%E1%B9%85kh%C4%81ra#Conditioned_things), which is *dukkha*, "incapable of satisfying" and painful.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENyanatiloka198065-76][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmmanuel201330-77][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams200274–75-78][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELopez2009147-79] This keeps us caught in [saṃsāra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sa%E1%B9%83s%C4%81ra_\(Buddhism\)), metaphysically interpreted as the endless cycle of repeated [rebirth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebirth_\(Buddhism\)), dukkha and dying again,[^cite_note-Samsara-84] also interpreted as a psychological cycle of repetitious rebirth of the ego.[^cite_note-Payutto-85][^cite_note-Buddhadasa-86]

But there is a way to [liberation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moksha) from this endless cycle[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWarder200045–46-87] to the state of [nirvana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nirvana), namely following the [Noble Eightfold Path](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noble_Eightfold_Path).[^cite_note-Moksha-101]

The truth of *[dukkha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dukkha)* is the basic insight that life in this mundane world, with its clinging and craving to [impermanent states and things](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sa%E1%B9%85kh%C4%81ra#Conditioned_things)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENyanatiloka198065-76] is *dukkha*, and unsatisfactory.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams200274–75-78][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELopez2009147-79][^cite_note-EB-DL_Four_Truths-94] *Dukkha* can be translated as "incapable of satisfying",[^cite_note-Sumedho-first-102] "the unsatisfactory nature and the general insecurity of all [conditioned phenomena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sa%E1%B9%85kh%C4%81ra#Conditioned_things)"; or "painful".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENyanatiloka198065-76][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmmanuel201330-77] *Dukkha* is most commonly translated as "suffering", but this is inaccurate, since it refers not to episodic suffering, but to the intrinsically unsatisfactory nature of temporary states and things, including pleasant but temporary experiences.[^cite_note-pain-12] We expect happiness from states and things which are impermanent, and therefore cannot attain real happiness.

The Four Noble Truths are:

- *[dukkha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dukkha)* ("not being at ease", "suffering", "pain") is an innate characteristic of the perpetual cycle (*[samsara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samsara)*, lit. 'wandering') of [grasping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Up%C4%81d%C4%81na) at things, ideas and habits

- *samudaya* (origin, arising, combination; "cause"): *dukkha* is caused by, or arises with, *[taṇhā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ta%E1%B9%87h%C4%81)* ("craving," "desire" or "attachment," literally "thirst")

- *[nirodha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nirodha)* (cessation, ending, confinement): *dukkha* can be ended or contained by the confinement or letting go of *taṇhā*

- *marga* (path, "way leading to the ending of pain") is the path leading to the confinement of *taṇhā* and *dukkha*, classically the [Noble Eightfold Path](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noble_Eightfold_Path) but also summarised in [other paths to liberation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_paths_to_liberation)

  [^cite_note-104]

### Three marks of existence

Main article: [Three marks of existence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_marks_of_existence)

Buddhism teaches that the idea that anything is permanent or that there is self in any being is ignorance or misperception (*[avijjā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avidy%C4%81_\(Buddhism\))*), and that this is the primary source of clinging and dukkha.[^cite_note-105][^cite_note-GombrichScherrer2008p209-106][^cite_note-HoffmanMahinda2013p162-107]

Ignorance is countered by insight (*[paññā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Praj%C3%B1%C4%81_\(Buddhism\))*); most schools of Buddhism, therefore, teach [three marks of existence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_marks_of_existence), which fundamentally characterise all phenomena:[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich2005a47,_Quote:_"All_phenomenal_existence_\[in_Buddhism\]_is_said_to_have_three_interlocking_characteristics:_impermanence,_suffering_and_lack_of_soul_or_essence."-108]

- *[Anicca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impermanence_\(Buddhism\))*: impermanence

- *[Dukkha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Du%E1%B8%A5kha)*: unsatisfactoriness

- *[Anattā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatt%C4%81)*: non-self; living things have no permanent immanent soul or essence

  [^cite_note-britannicaanatta-109]

  [^cite_note-110]

  [^cite_note-5sourcesanatta-111]

Some schools describe four characteristics or "four seals of the Dharma", adding to the above

- [Nirvana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nirvana) is peaceful/peace (*śānta/śānti*)

  [^cite_note-112]

  [^cite_note-113]

### The cycle of rebirth



Traditional [Tibetan Buddhist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibetan_Buddhism) [Thangka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thangka) depicting the [Wheel of Life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhavacakra) with its six realms

#### Saṃsāra

Main article: [Saṃsāra (Buddhism)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sa%E1%B9%83s%C4%81ra_\(Buddhism\))

*Saṃsāra* means "wandering" or "world", with the connotation of cyclic, circuitous change.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlostermaier2010604-114][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJuergensmeyerRoof2011271–272-115] It refers to the theory of rebirth and "cyclicality of all life, matter, existence", a fundamental assumption of Buddhism, as with all major Indian religions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJuergensmeyerRoof2011271–272-115][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETrainor200458,_Quote:_"Buddhism_shares_with_Hinduism_the_doctrine_of_Samsara,_whereby_all_beings_pass_through_an_unceasing_cycle_of_birth,_death_and_rebirth_until_they_find_a_means_of_liberation_from_the_cycle._However,_Buddhism_differs_from_Hinduism_in_rejecting_the_assertion_that_every_human_being_possesses_a_changeless_soul_which_constitutes_his_or_her_ultimate_identity,_and_which_transmigrates_from_one_incarnation_to_the_next.-116] Samsara in Buddhism is considered to be *[dukkha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dukkha)*, unsatisfactory and painful,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson2010-117] perpetuated by desire and *[avidya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avidy%C4%81_\(Buddhism\))* (ignorance), and the resulting [karma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karma).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJuergensmeyerRoof2011271–272-115][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcClelland2010172,_240-118][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliamsTribeWynne201218–19,_chapter_1-119] Liberation from this cycle of existence, *nirvana*, has been the foundation and the most important historical justification of Buddhism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConze201371,_Quote:_"Nirvana_is_the_''raison_d'être''_of_Buddhism,_and_its_ultimate_justification."-120][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGethin1998119-121]

Buddhist texts assert that rebirth can occur in six realms of existence, namely three good realms (heavenly, demi-god, human) and three evil realms (animal, hungry ghosts, hellish).[^cite_note-realms2-123] Samsara ends if a person attains [nirvana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nirvana), the "blowing out" of the afflictions through insight into [impermanence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impermanence) and "[non-self](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatta)".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBuswellGimello19927–8,_83–84-124][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChoong199928–29,_Quote:_"Seeing_(''passati'')_the_nature_of_things_as_impermanent_leads_to_the_removal_of_the_view_of_self,_and_so_to_the_realisation_of_nirvana."-125][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERahula201451–58-126]

#### Rebirth

Main article: [Rebirth (Buddhism)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebirth_\(Buddhism\))

A very large hill behind two palm trees and a boulevard, where the Buddha is believed to have been cremated

Ramabhar Stupa in [Kushinagar](33686220.md), Uttar Pradesh, India, is regionally believed to be Buddha's cremation site.

Rebirth refers to a process whereby beings go through a succession of lifetimes as one of many possible forms of [sentient life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sentient_beings_\(Buddhism\)), each running from conception to death.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeown1996107-127] In Buddhist thought, this rebirth does not involve a [soul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soul) or any fixed substance. This is because the Buddhist doctrine of [anattā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatta) (Sanskrit: *anātman*, no-self doctrine) rejects the concepts of a permanent self or an unchanging, eternal soul found in other religions.[^cite_note-Leaman2002p23-128][^cite_note-129]

The Buddhist traditions have traditionally disagreed on what it is in a person that is reborn, as well as how quickly the rebirth occurs after death.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBuswellLopez2003708–709-130][^cite_note-Neufeldt1986p123-131] Some Buddhist traditions assert that "no self" doctrine means that there is no enduring self, but there is *avacya* (inexpressible) personality (*[pudgala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pudgalavada)*) which migrates from one life to another.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBuswellLopez2003708–709-130] The majority of Buddhist traditions, in contrast, assert that [vijñāna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vij%C3%B1%C4%81na) (a person's consciousness) though evolving, exists as a continuum and is the mechanistic basis of what undergoes the rebirth process.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams200274–75-78][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBuswellLopez2003708–709-130] The quality of one's rebirth depends on the [merit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_\(Buddhism\)) or demerit gained by one's karma (i.e., actions), as well as that accrued on one's behalf by a family member.[^cite_note-133] Buddhism also developed [a complex cosmology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_cosmology) to explain the various realms or planes of rebirth.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson2010-117]

#### Karma

Main article: [Karma in Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karma_in_Buddhism)

[In Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karma_in_Buddhism), karma (from [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit): "action, work") drives *[saṃsāra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sa%E1%B9%83s%C4%81ra)*—the endless cycle of suffering and rebirth for each being. Good, skilful deeds (Pāli: *kusala*) and bad, unskilful deeds (Pāli: *akusala*) produce "seeds" in the unconscious receptacle (*ālaya*) that mature later either in this life or in a subsequent [rebirth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebirth_\(Buddhism\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2013131,_32–34-134][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKasulis20061–12-135] The existence of karma is a core belief in Buddhism, as with all major Indian religions, and it implies neither fatalism nor that everything that happens to a person is caused by karma.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201340–41-136] (Diseases and suffering induced by the disruptive actions of other people are examples of non-karma suffering.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201340–41-136])

A central aspect of Buddhist theory of karma is that intent (*[cetanā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cetan%C4%81)*) matters and is essential to bring about a consequence or *[phala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phala)* "fruit" or [vipāka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vip%C4%81ka) "result".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKrishan199759–78-137] The emphasis on intent in Buddhism marks a difference from the karmic theory of Jainism, where karma accumulates with or without intent.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201340-138][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKrishan199747,_55-139] The emphasis on intent is also found in Hinduism, and Buddhism may have influenced karma theories of Hinduism.[^cite_note-140]

In Buddhism, good or bad karma accumulates even if there is no physical action, and just having ill or good thoughts creates karmic seeds; thus, actions of body, speech or mind all lead to karmic seeds.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201340–41-136] In the Buddhist traditions, life aspects affected by the law of karma in past and current births of a being include the form of rebirth, realm of rebirth, social class, character and major circumstances of a lifetime.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201340–41-136][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpiro1982430_with_footnote_1-141][^cite_note-142] According to the theory, it operates like the laws of physics, without external intervention, on every being in all [six realms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samsara_\(Buddhism\)) of existence including human beings and gods.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201340–41-136][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELopez2001239–248-143]

A notable aspect of the karma theory in modern Buddhism is merit transfer.[^cite_note-appletonp129-144][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpiro1982124–128-145] A person accumulates merit not only through intentions and ethical living, but also is able to gain merit from others by exchanging goods and services, such as through *dāna* (charity to monks or nuns).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201345–46-146] The theory also states a person can transfer one's own good karma to living family members and ancestors.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpiro1982124–128-145]

This Buddhist idea may have roots in the *quid-pro-quo* exchange beliefs of the Hindu Vedic rituals.[^cite_note-Egge2013-147] The "karma merit transfer" concept has been controversial, not accepted in later Jainism and Hinduism traditions, unlike Buddhism where it was adopted in ancient times and remains a common practice.[^cite_note-appletonp129-144] According to Bruce Reichenbach, the "merit transfer" idea was generally absent in early Buddhism and may have emerged with the rise of Mahayana Buddhism; he adds that while major Hindu schools such as Yoga, Advaita Vedanta and others do not believe in merit transfer, some bhakti Hindu traditions later adopted the idea just like Buddhism.[^cite_note-148]

### Liberation

Main articles: [Moksha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moksha) and [Nirvana (Buddhism)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nirvana_\(Buddhism\))



An [aniconic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aniconism) depiction of the Buddha's spiritual liberation (*moksha*) or awakening (*bodhi*), at [Sanchi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanchi). The Buddha is not depicted, only symbolised by the Bodhi tree and the empty seat

The cessation of the *[kleshas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kleshas_\(Buddhism\))* and the attainment of [nirvana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nirvana) (*nibbāna*), with which the cycle of rebirth ends, has been the primary and the [soteriological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soteriological) goal of the Buddhist path for monastic life since the time of the Buddha.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESamuel2008136-89][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBuswellLopez2003589–590-149][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECollins1998135–177,_188,_443-150] The term "path" is usually taken to mean the [Noble Eightfold Path](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noble_Eightfold_Path), but [other versions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_Paths_to_liberation) of "the path" can also be found in the Nikayas.[^cite_note-152] In some passages in the Pali Canon, a distinction is being made between right knowledge or insight (*sammā-ñāṇa*), and right liberation or release (*sammā-vimutti*), as the means to attain cessation and liberation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChoong2000141-153][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFuller200555–56-154]

Nirvana literally means "blowing out, quenching, becoming extinguished".[^cite_note-Collins2010p63-155][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECousins19969-156] In early Buddhist texts, it is the state of restraint and self-control that leads to the "blowing out" and the ending of the cycles of sufferings associated with rebirths and redeaths.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVetter1988-157][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich199766-158][^cite_note-159] Many later Buddhist texts describe nirvana as identical with *[anatta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatta)* with complete "emptiness, nothingness".[^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161][^cite_note-162][^cite_note-164] In some texts, the state is described with greater detail, such as passing through the gate of emptiness (*sunyata*)—realising that there is no soul or self in any living being, then passing through the gate of signlessness (*animitta*)—realising that nirvana cannot be perceived, and finally passing through the gate of wishlessness (*apranihita*)—realising that nirvana is the state of not even wishing for nirvana.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBuswellLopez2003589–590-149][^cite_note-165][^cite_note-167]

The nirvana state has been described in Buddhist texts partly in a manner similar to other Indian religions, as the state of complete liberation, enlightenment, highest happiness, bliss, fearlessness, freedom, permanence, non-dependent origination, unfathomable, and indescribable.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECollins1998191–233-168][^cite_note-169] It has also been described in part differently, as a state of spiritual release marked by "emptiness" and realisation of *[non-self](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatta)*.[^cite_note-170][^cite_note-171][^cite_note-172][^cite_note-175]

While Buddhism considers the liberation from [saṃsāra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sa%E1%B9%83s%C4%81ra_\(Buddhism\)) as the ultimate spiritual goal, in traditional practice, the primary focus of a vast majority of lay Buddhists has been to seek and accumulate merit through good deeds, donations to monks and various Buddhist rituals in order to gain better rebirths rather than nirvana.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECoogan2003192-176][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETrainor200462-177][^cite_note-180]

### Dependent arising

Main articles: [Pratītyasamutpāda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prat%C4%ABtyasamutp%C4%81da) and [Twelve Nidānas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve_Nid%C4%81nas)

*Pratityasamutpada*, also called "dependent arising, or dependent origination", is the Buddhist theory to explain the nature and relations of being, becoming, existence and ultimate reality. Buddhism asserts that there is nothing independent, except the state of nirvana.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey199854-181] All physical and mental states depend on and arise from other pre-existing states, and in turn from them arise other dependent states while they cease.[^cite_note-182]

The 'dependent arisings' have a causal conditioning, and thus *Pratityasamutpada* is the Buddhist belief that causality is the basis of [ontology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ontology), not a creator God nor the ontological Vedic concept called universal Self ([Brahman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahman)) nor any other 'transcendent creative principle'.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams200264,_Quote:_In_the_''Mahatanhasankhaya_Sutta''_the_Buddha_\[stresses\]_that_things_originate_in_dependence_upon_causal_conditioning,_and_this_emphasis_on_causality_describes_the_central_feature_of_Buddhist_ontology._All_elements_of_samsara_exist_in_some_sense_or_another_relative_to_their_causes_and_conditions.-183][^cite_note-184] However, Buddhist thought does not understand causality in terms of Newtonian mechanics; rather it understands it as conditioned arising.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGethin1998153–155-185][^cite_note-186] In Buddhism, dependent arising refers to conditions created by a plurality of causes that necessarily co-originate a phenomenon within and across lifetimes, such as karma in one life creating conditions that lead to rebirth in one of the realms of existence for another lifetime.[^cite_note-Kalupahana1975p54-187][^cite_note-188][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGethin1998151–152-189]

Buddhism applies the theory of dependent arising to explain origination of endless cycles of *dukkha* and rebirth, through [Twelve Nidānas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve_Nid%C4%81nas) or "twelve links". It states that because [Avidyā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avidy%C4%81_\(Buddhism\)) (ignorance) exists, [Saṃskāras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sa%E1%B9%85kh%C4%81ra) (karmic formations) exist; because Saṃskāras exist therefore [Vijñāna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vij%C3%B1%C4%81na) (consciousness) exists; and in a similar manner it links [Nāmarūpa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N%C4%81mar%C5%ABpa) (the sentient body), [Ṣaḍāyatana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E1%B9%A2a%E1%B8%8D%C4%81yatana) (our six senses), [Sparśa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spar%C5%9Ba) (sensory stimulation), [Vedanā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vedan%C4%81) (feeling), [Taṇhā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ta%E1%B9%87h%C4%81) (craving), [Upādāna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Up%C4%81d%C4%81na) (grasping), [Bhava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhava) (becoming), [Jāti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C4%81ti_\(Buddhism\)) (birth), and [Jarāmaraṇa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jar%C4%81mara%E1%B9%87a) (old age, death, sorrow, and pain).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201365–72-190][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmmanuel201351–66-191] By breaking the circuitous links of the Twelve Nidanas, Buddhism asserts that liberation from these endless cycles of rebirth and dukkha can be attained.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey199854,_Quote:_"The_main_concrete_application_of_the_abstract_principle_is_in_the_form_of_a_series_of_conditioned_links_(''nidanas''),_culminating_in_the_arising_of_dukkha."_(...)_"This_\[doctrine\]_states_the_principle_of_conditionality,_that_all_things,_mental_and_physical,_arise_and_exist_due_to_the_presence_of_certain_conditions,_and_cease_once_their_conditions_are_removed:_nothing_(except_''Nibbana'')_is_independent._The_doctrine_thus_complements_the_teaching_that_no_permanent,_independent_self_can_be_found."-192]

### Not-Self and Emptiness

Main articles: [Anātman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/An%C4%81tman) and [Śūnyatā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9A%C5%ABnyat%C4%81)

| The **[Five Aggregates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skandha)** (*pañcakkhandha*) according to the [Pali Canon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pali_Canon). |  |  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  |  |  |  |  |
|  | [**form** (*rūpa*)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R%C5%ABpa)     [4 elements (*mahābhūta*)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mah%C4%81bh%C5%ABta)             ↓       [contact (*phassa*)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spar%C5%9Ba)       ↓ ↑    [**consciousness** (*viññāna*)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vij%C3%B1%C4%81na) | → ←    ← | [mental factors (*cetasika*)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mental_factors_\(Buddhism\))      [**feeling** (*vedanā*)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vedan%C4%81)        [**perception** (*sañña*)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sa%E1%B9%83j%C3%B1%C4%81)        [**formation** (*_saṅkhāra_*)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sa%E1%B9%85kh%C4%81ra) |  |
|  |  |  |  |  |
| [Form](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R%C5%ABpa) is derived from the [Four Great Elements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahabhuta). [Consciousness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vij%C3%B1%C4%81na) arises from [other aggregates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Namarupa). Mental Factors arise from the [Contact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phassa) of [Consciousness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vinnana) and [other aggregates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Namarupa). The five aggregates are [no-self](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatt%C4%81). |  |  |  |  |
| [Source: MN 109 (Thanissaro, 2001)](http://www.accesstoinsight.org/tipitaka/mn/mn.109.than.html)  \|  [diagram details](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template\:PancaKhandha) |  |  |  |  |

A related doctrine in Buddhism is that of *anattā* (Pali) or *anātman* (Sanskrit). It is the view that there is no unchanging, permanent self, soul or essence in phenomena.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich200647-193] The Buddha and Buddhist philosophers who follow him such as Vasubandhu and Buddhaghosa, generally argue for this view by analysing the person through the schema of the [five aggregates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_aggregates), and then attempting to show that none of these five components of personality can be permanent or absolute.[^cite_note-194] This can be seen in Buddhist discourses such as the *[Anattalakkhana Sutta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anattalakkhana_Sutta)*.

"Emptiness" or "voidness" (Sk&#x74;*: Śūnyatā*, Pali: *Suññatā)*, is a related concept with many different interpretations throughout the various Buddhisms. In early Buddhism, it was commonly stated that all five aggregates are void (*rittaka*), hollow (*tucchaka*), coreless (*asāraka*), for example as in the *Pheṇapiṇḍūpama Sutta* (SN 22\:95).[^cite_note-195] Similarly, in Theravada Buddhism, it often means that the five aggregates are empty of a Self.[^cite_note-196]

Emptiness is a central concept in Mahāyāna Buddhism, especially in [Nagarjuna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nagarjuna)'s [Madhyamaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madhyamaka) school, and in the *[Prajñāpāramitā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prajnaparamita) sutras*. In Madhyamaka philosophy, emptiness is the view which holds that all phenomena are without any *[svabhava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Svabhava)* (literally "own-nature" or "self-nature"), and are thus without any underlying essence, and so are "empty" of being independent. This doctrine sought to refute the heterodox theories of *svabhava* circulating at the time.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELindtner1997324-197]

### The Three Jewels

Main article: [Three Jewels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_Jewels)

All forms of Buddhism revere and take spiritual refuge in the "three jewels" (*triratna*): Buddha, Dharma and Sangha.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2013244–245-198]

#### Buddha

Main article: [Buddhahood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhahood)

While all varieties of Buddhism revere "Buddha" and "buddhahood", they have different views on what these are. Regardless of their interpretation, the concept of Buddha is central to all forms of Buddhism.

In Theravada Buddhism, a Buddha is someone who has become awake through their own efforts and insight. They have put an end to their cycle of rebirths and have ended all unwholesome mental states which lead to bad action and thus are morally perfected.[^cite_note-Crosby,_Kate_2013_p._16-199] While subject to the limitations of the human body in certain ways (for example, in the early texts, the Buddha suffers from backaches), a Buddha is said to be "deep, immeasurable, hard-to-fathom as is the great ocean", and also has immense psychic powers ([abhijñā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abhij%C3%B1%C4%81)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201327–28-200]

Mahāyāna Buddhism meanwhile, has a vastly expanded [cosmology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_cosmology), with various [Buddhas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhahood) and other holy beings (*aryas*) residing in different realms. Mahāyāna texts not only revere numerous [Buddhas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_deities#Buddhas) besides [Shakyamuni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Buddha), such as [Amitabha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amit%C4%81bha) and [Vairocana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vairochana), but also see them as transcendental or supramundane (*lokuttara*) beings.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams200821-201] Mahāyāna Buddhism holds that these other Buddhas in other realms can be contacted and are able to benefit beings in this world.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2013162-202] In Mahāyāna, a Buddha is a kind of "spiritual king", a "protector of all creatures" with a lifetime that is countless of eons long, rather than just a human teacher who has transcended the world after death.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams200827-203] Shakyamuni's life and death on earth is then usually understood as a "mere appearance" or "a manifestation skilfully projected into earthly life by a long-enlightened transcendent being, who is still available to teach the faithful through visionary experiences".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams200827-203][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2013164-204]

#### Dharma

Main article: [Dharma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharma)

The second of the three jewels is "Dharma" (Pali: Dhamma), which in Buddhism refers to the Buddha's teaching, which includes all of the main ideas outlined above. While this teaching reflects the true nature of reality, it is not a belief to be clung to, but a pragmatic teaching to be put into practice. It is likened to a raft which is "for crossing over" (to nirvana) not for holding on to.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201331-205] It also refers to the universal law and cosmic order which that teaching both reveals and relies upon.[^cite_note-206] It is an everlasting principle which applies to all beings and worlds. In that sense it is also the ultimate truth and reality about the universe, it is thus "the way that things really are".

#### Sangha

Main articles: [Sangha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sangha), [Bodhisattva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bodhisattva), and [Arhat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arhat)



Buddhist monks and nuns praying in the [Buddha Tooth Relic Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddha_Tooth_Relic_Temple) of Singapore

The third "jewel" which Buddhists take refuge in is the "Sangha", which refers to the monastic community of monks and nuns who follow Gautama Buddha's monastic discipline which was "designed to shape the Sangha as an ideal community, with the optimum conditions for spiritual growth."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201388-207] The Sangha consists of those who have chosen to follow the Buddha's ideal way of life, which is one of celibate monastic renunciation with minimal material possessions (such as an alms bowl and robes).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGethin199885–88-208]

The Sangha is seen as important because they preserve and pass down Buddha Dharma. As Gethin states "the Sangha lives the teaching, preserves the teaching as Scriptures and teaches the wider community. Without the Sangha there is no Buddhism."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGethin199892-209] The Sangha also acts as a "field of merit" for laypersons, allowing them to make spiritual merit or goodness by donating to the Sangha and supporting them. In return, they keep their duty to preserve and spread the Dharma everywhere for the good of the world.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGethin199886-210] Monasteries earn this trust in practice through "contractural" documents that function like an agreement between the monastery's leaders, its monks and nuns, and the lay community, including the donors that support it.[^cite_note-211]

There is also a separate definition of Sangha, referring to those who have attained any [stage of awakening](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_stages_of_enlightenment), whether or not they are monastics. This sangha is called the [*āryasaṅgha*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Ar%C4%81vaka#The_community_of_disciples) "noble Sangha".[^cite_note-212] All forms of Buddhism generally reveres these *[āryas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arya_\(Buddhism\))* (Pali: *ariya*, "noble ones" or "holy ones") who are spiritually attained beings. Aryas have attained the fruits of the Buddhist path.[^cite_note-213]

### Other key Mahāyāna views

Main articles: [Yogachara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yogachara) and [Buddha-nature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddha-nature)

Mahāyāna Buddhism also differs from Theravada and the other schools of early Buddhism in promoting several unique doctrines which are contained in Mahāyāna sutras and philosophical treatises.

One of these is the unique interpretation of emptiness and dependent origination found in the Madhyamaka school. Another very influential doctrine for Mahāyāna is the main philosophical view of the [Yogācāra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yogachara) school variously, termed *Vijñaptimātratā-vāda* ("the doctrine that there are only ideas" or "mental impressions") or *Vijñānavāda* ("the doctrine of consciousness"). According to Mark Siderits, what classical Yogācāra thinkers like Vasubandhu had in mind is that we are only ever aware of mental images or impressions, which may appear as external objects, but "there is actually no such thing outside the mind".[^cite_note-214] There are several interpretations of this main theory, many scholars see it as a type of Idealism, others as a kind of phenomenology.[^cite_note-215]

Another very influential concept unique to Mahāyāna is that of "Buddha-nature" (*buddhadhātu*) or "Tathagata-womb" (*tathāgatagarbha*). Buddha-nature is a concept found in some 1st-millennium CE Buddhist texts, such as the *[Tathāgatagarbha sūtras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tath%C4%81gatagarbha_s%C5%ABtras)*. According to Paul Williams these [Sutras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sutra) suggest that 'all sentient beings contain a Tathagata' as their 'essence, core inner nature, Self'.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams2008104-216][^cite_note-218] According to Karl Brunnhölzl "the earliest mahayana sutras that are based on and discuss the notion of tathāgatagarbha as the buddha potential that is innate in all sentient beings began to appear in written form in the late second and early third century."[^cite_note-219] For some, the doctrine seems to conflict with the Buddhist anatta doctrine (non-Self), leading scholars to posit that the *Tathāgatagarbha Sutras* were written to promote Buddhism to non-Buddhists.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams2008104–105,_108–109,_Quote:_"..._\[The_''Mahaparinirvana_Sutra''\]_refers_to_the_Buddha_using_the_term_"Self"_in_order_to_win_over_non-Buddhist_ascetics."-220][^cite_note-221] This can be seen in texts like the *[Laṅkāvatāra Sūtra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La%E1%B9%85k%C4%81vat%C4%81ra_S%C5%ABtra)*, which state that Buddha-nature is taught to help those who have fear when they listen to the teaching of anatta.[^cite_note-222] Buddhist texts like the *[Ratnagotravibhāga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ratnagotravibh%C4%81ga)* clarify that the "Self" implied in *Tathagatagarbha* doctrine is actually "[not-self](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatt%C4%81)".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams2008112-223][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHookham199196-224]

## Paths to liberation

Main article: [Buddhist paths to liberation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_paths_to_liberation)

The *[Bodhipakkhiyādhammā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bodhipakkhiy%C4%81dhamm%C4%81)* are seven lists of qualities or factors that promote spiritual awakening (*bodhi*). Each list is a short summary of the Buddhist path, and the seven lists substantially overlap. The best-known list in the West is the [Noble Eightfold Path](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noble_Eightfold_Path), but a wide variety of paths and models of progress have been used and described in the different Buddhist traditions. However, they generally share basic practices such as *sila* (ethics), *samadhi* (meditation, *dhyana*) and *prajña* (wisdom), which are known as the three trainings. An important additional practice is a kind and compassionate attitude toward every living being and the world. [Devotion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_devotion) is also important in some Buddhist traditions, and in the Tibetan traditions visualisations of deities and mandalas are important. The value of textual study is regarded differently in the various Buddhist traditions. It is central to Theravada and highly important to Tibetan Buddhism, while the Zen tradition takes an ambiguous stance.

An important guiding principle of Buddhist practice is the [Middle Way](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Way) (*madhyamapratipad*). It was a part of Buddha's first sermon, where he presented the [Noble Eightfold Path](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noble_Eightfold_Path) that was a 'middle way' between the extremes of asceticism and hedonistic sense pleasures.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201323,_81-13][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeown199624,_59-14][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBronkhorst2011233–237-15][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchuhmacherWoener1991143-16] In Buddhism, states Harvey, the doctrine of "dependent arising" (conditioned arising, *pratītyasamutpāda*) to explain rebirth is viewed as the 'middle way' between the doctrines that a being has a "permanent soul" involved in rebirth (eternalism) and "death is final and there is no rebirth" (annihilationism).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201372-17][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBuswellLopez200349,_''antagrahadrsti''-18]

### Paths to liberation in the early texts

A common presentation style of the path (*mārga*) to liberation in the [Early Buddhist Texts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Buddhist_Texts) is the "graduated talk", in which the Buddha lays out a step-by-step training.[^cite_note-225]

In the early texts, numerous different sequences of the gradual path can be found.[^cite_note-Bucknell1984-226] One of the most important and widely used presentations among the various Buddhist schools is The [Noble Eightfold Path](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noble_Eightfold_Path), or "Eightfold Path of the Noble Ones" (Skt. *'āryāṣṭāṅgamārga'*). This can be found in various discourses, most famously in the *[Dhammacakkappavattana Sutta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhammacakkappavattana_Sutta)* (The discourse on the turning of the [Dharma wheel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharma_Wheel)).

Other suttas such as the *Tevijja Sutta*, and the *Cula-Hatthipadopama-sutta* give a different outline of the path, though with many similar elements such as ethics and meditation.[^cite_note-Bucknell1984-226]

According to Rupert Gethin, the path to awakening is also frequently summarised by another a short formula: "abandoning the hindrances, practice of the four establishings of mindfulness, and development of the awakening factors".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGethin2001xiii–xiv-227]

#### Noble Eightfold Path

Main article: [Noble Eightfold Path](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noble_Eightfold_Path)

The Eightfold Path consists of a set of eight interconnected factors or conditions, that when developed together, lead to the cessation of [dukkha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dukkha).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAjahn_Sucitto201087–88-228] These eight factors are: Right View (or Right Understanding), Right Intention (or Right Thought), Right Speech, Right Action, Right Livelihood, Right Effort, Right Mindfulness, and Right Concentration.

This Eightfold Path is the fourth of the [Four Noble Truths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Noble_Truths) and asserts the path to the cessation of *dukkha* (suffering, pain, unsatisfactoriness).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGethin199881–83-229][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnderson201364–65-230] The path teaches that the way of the enlightened ones stopped their craving, clinging and [karmic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karma) accumulations, and thus ended their endless cycles of rebirth and suffering.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2016253–255-231][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBodhi20101–13-232][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliamsTribeWynne201252-233]

The Noble Eightfold Path is grouped into [three basic divisions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_disciplines_of_Buddhism), as follows:[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVetter198812–13-234][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201383–85-235][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBodhi201047–48-236]

| Division | Eightfold factor | *Sanskrit, Pali* | Description |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Wisdom (Sanskrit: *[prajñā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisdom_in_Buddhism)*, Pāli: *paññā*) | 1. Right view | *samyag dṛṣṭi, sammā ditthi* | The belief that there is an afterlife and not everything ends with death, that Buddha taught and followed a successful path to nirvana;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVetter198812–13-234] according to Peter Harvey, the right view is held in Buddhism as a belief in the Buddhist principles of [karma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karma) and [rebirth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebirth_\(Buddhism\)), and the importance of the [Four Noble Truths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Noble_Truths) and the True Realities.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201383–84-237] |
| 2. Right intention | *samyag saṃkalpa, sammā saṅkappa* | Giving up home and adopting the life of a religious mendicant in order to follow the path;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVetter198812–13-234] this concept, states Harvey, aims at peaceful renunciation, into an environment of non-sensuality, non-ill-will (to lovingkindness), away from cruelty (to compassion).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201383–84-237] |  |
| Moral virtues[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201383–85-235] (Sanskrit: *[śīla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9A%C4%ABla)*, Pāli: *sīla*) | 3. Right speech | *samyag vāc, sammā vāca* | No lying, no rude speech, no telling one person what another says about him, speaking that which leads to salvation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVetter198812–13-234] |
| 4. Right action | *samyag karman, sammā kammanta* | No killing or injuring, no taking what is not given; no sexual acts in monastic pursuit,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVetter198812–13-234] for lay Buddhists no sensual misconduct such as sexual involvement with someone married, or with an unmarried woman protected by her parents or relatives.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGowans2013440-238][^cite_note-239][^cite_note-240] |  |
| 5. Right livelihood | *samyag ājīvana, sammā ājīva* | For monks, beg to feed, only possessing what is essential to sustain life.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVetter198812-241] For lay Buddhists, the canonical texts state right livelihood as abstaining from wrong livelihood, explained as not becoming a source or means of suffering to sentient beings by cheating them, or harming or killing them in any way.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201383,_273–274-242][^cite_note-243] |  |
| Meditation[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201383–85-235] (Sanskrit and Pāli: *[samādhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam%C4%81dhi)*) | 6. Right effort | *samyag vyāyāma, sammā vāyāma* | Guard against sensual thoughts; this concept, states Harvey, aims at preventing unwholesome states that disrupt meditation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201383-244] |
| 7. Right mindfulness | *samyag smṛti, sammā sati* | Never be absent-minded, conscious of what one is doing; this, states Harvey, encourages mindfulness about impermanence of the body, feelings and mind, as well as to experience the five [skandhas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skandha), the five hindrances, the four True Realities and seven factors of awakening.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201383-244] |  |
| 8. Right concentration | *samyag samādhi, sammā samādhi* | Correct meditation or concentration (*dhyana*), explained as the four jhānas.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVetter198812–13-234][^cite_note-bucknellkangp12-245] |  |

## Common practices



[Buddha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddha)'s sermon in the [Sarnath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarnath), depicted at [Wat Chedi Liam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wat_Chedi_Liam), near [Chiang Mai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiang_Mai), Thailand

### Hearing and learning the Dharma

In various suttas which present the graduated path taught by the Buddha, such as the *[Samaññaphala Sutta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sama%C3%B1%C3%B1aphala_Sutta)* and the *Cula-Hatthipadopama Sutta,* the first step on the path is hearing the Buddha teach the Dharma. This then said to lead to the acquiring of confidence or faith in the Buddha's teachings.[^cite_note-Bucknell1984-226]

Mahayana Buddhist teachers such as [Yin Shun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yin_Shun) also state that hearing the Dharma and study of the Buddhist discourses is necessary "if one wants to learn and practice the Buddha Dharma."[^cite_note-246] Likewise, in Indo-Tibetan Buddhism, the "Stages of the Path" (*Lamrim*) texts generally place the activity of listening to the Buddhist teachings as an important early practice.[^cite_note-247]

### Refuge

Main article: [Refuge (Buddhism)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refuge_\(Buddhism\))

Traditionally, the first step in most Buddhist schools requires taking of the "Three Refuges", also called the Three Jewels ([Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit): *triratna*, [Pali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pali): *tiratana*) as the foundation of one's religious practice.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2013249-248] This practice may have been influenced by the Brahmanical motif of the triple refuge, found in the *[Rigveda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rigveda)* 9.97.47, *Rigveda* 6.46.9 and *[Chandogya Upanishad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chandogya_Upanishad)* 2.22.3–4.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShults2014108-249] Tibetan Buddhism sometimes adds a fourth refuge, in the *[lama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lama)*. The three refuges are believed by Buddhists to be protective and a form of reverence.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2013249-248]

The ancient formula which is repeated for taking refuge affirms that "I go to the Buddha as refuge, I go to the Dhamma as refuge, I go to the Sangha as refuge."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2013244-250] Reciting the three refuges, according to Harvey, is considered not as a place to hide, rather a thought that "purifies, uplifts and strengthens the heart".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2013244–245-198]

### Devotional and liturgical practices

Main articles: [Buddhist devotion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_devotion), [Buddhist liturgy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_liturgy), and [Pūjā (Buddhism)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C5%ABj%C4%81_\(Buddhism\))



A [Jōdo-shū](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C5%8Ddo-sh%C5%AB) priest in [Fukui prefecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fukui_Prefecture) (Japan) chanting on behalf of a deceased parishioner at their family home.&#x20;

Most forms of Buddhism consider *[saddhā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faith_in_Buddhism)* (Sanskrit: *śraddhā,* 'trustful confidence' or 'faith'), as a key quality on the path that prepares one for meditative insights.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2013237-251] Furthermore, the related quality of devotion (Sanskrit: [bhakti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhakti); Pali: bhatti) is an important part of the path of most Buddhists, since it supports wholesome qualities like faith and peace of mind.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey1998170-252] Buddhist devotional and liturgical practices encompass a wide range of forms oriented toward honoring the Buddha, cultivating wholesome mental states, and structuring communal religious life. [Buddhist devotional practices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_devotion) include [Buddhist liturgy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_liturgy), ritual worship ([pūjā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C5%ABj%C4%81_\(Buddhism\))), [pilgrimage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pilgrimage_\(Buddhism\)), [prostrations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prostration_\(Buddhism\)), along with [Buddhist music and chanting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_music).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETrainor200484–85,_105,_108–109,_112–113,_116,_165,_185-253]

As in Hinduism, pūjās typically involves offerings of flowers, incense, lamps, food, or water on a [Buddhist altar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Butsudan) along with certain recitations and physical acts like bowing. Such rites are understood to generate [merit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_\(Buddhism\)), cultivate wholesome states of mind (such as a state of [refuge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refuge_in_Buddhism)) and support contemplation.[^cite_note-254] In formal temple settings, worship often follows complex liturgies, including the taking of refuge, precepts and vows, whereas more informal contexts may integrate simpler forms of homage and recitation. Buddhist devotional practices are usually focused on some object, image or location that is seen as holy or spiritually influential. Examples of objects of devotion (known as [honzon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honzon) in Japanese) include paintings or [statues of Buddhas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddha_in_art) and bodhisattvas, [stupas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stupa), sacred texts, and bodhi trees.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2013239–240-255]

Chanting practices constitute a major component of Buddhist devotion common to all Buddhist traditions going back to ancient India where chanting aided in the [memorisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memorization) of orally transmitted teachings.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2013243-256] A common practice is to chant or recite Buddhist sutras. Other chants are shorter formulas, mantras or [dharanis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharani). Among the most popular chants in Buddhism are those associated with [recollection of the Buddha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddh%C4%81nusm%E1%B9%9Bti) and refuge. In [East Asian Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Asian_Buddhism) this includes the widely popular [nianfo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nianfo) formula which is devoted to [Amitābha Buddha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amit%C4%81bha) and is central to [Pure Land Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pure_Land_Buddhism). In Himalayan Buddhism, the [Mani mantra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Om_mani_padme_hum) is a similarly popular chant among lay and ordained Buddhists alike. A common method of chanting is to use rosaries called [malas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japamala) to count repetitions. [Buddhist chanting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_music) can thus be a group or private devotional practice which leads to [tranquility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samatha-vipassan%C4%81), [faith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faith_in_Buddhism), accumulation of [merit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_\(Buddhism\)) (which can then be [transferred on behalf of others](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transfer_of_merit)), and memorization of the teachings.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2013243–244-257] In Mahayana traditions, especially in the Pure Land schools, chanting practice can also be seen as a way to stimulate or receive the [blessings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adhi%E1%B9%A3%E1%B9%ADh%C4%81na) and spiritual power (also known as [other-power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Other_power)) of the Buddhas and bodhisattvas.[^cite_note-258]

### *Śīla* – Buddhist ethics

Main article: [Buddhist ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_ethics)



Buddhist monks collect alms in [Si Phan Don](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Si_Phan_Don), Laos. Giving is a key virtue in Buddhism.

*Śīla* (Sanskrit) or *sīla* (Pāli) is the concept of "moral virtues", that is the second group and an integral part of the Noble Eightfold Path.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201383–84-237] It generally consists of right speech, right action and right livelihood.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201383–84-237]

One of the most basic forms of ethics in Buddhism is the taking of "precepts". This includes the Five Precepts for laypeople, Eight or Ten Precepts for monastic life, as well as rules of Dhamma (*Vinaya* or *Patimokkha*) adopted by a monastery.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams2005c398-259][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcFarlane2001187–193-260]

Other important elements of Buddhist ethics include [giving or charity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C4%81na) (*dāna*), [Mettā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mett%C4%81) (Good-Will), Heedfulness ([Appamada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appamada)), 'self-respect' ([Hri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hri_\(Buddhism\))) and 'regard for consequences' ([Apatrapya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apatrapya)).

#### Precepts

Main article: [Five precepts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_precepts)

Buddhist scriptures explain the five precepts ([Pali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pali_language): *pañcasīla*; [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit_language): *pañcaśīla*) as the minimal standard of Buddhist morality.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGowans2013440-238] It is the most important system of morality in Buddhism, together with the [monastic rules](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patimokkha).[^cite_note-Goodman-261]

The five precepts are seen as a basic training applicable to all Buddhists. They are:[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams2005c398-259][^cite_note-262][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey200067-263]

1. "I undertake the training-precept (*sikkha-padam*) to abstain from onslaught on breathing beings." This includes ordering or causing someone else to kill. The Pali suttas also say one should not "approve of others killing" and that one should be "scrupulous, compassionate, trembling for the welfare of all living beings".

   [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey200069-264]

2. "I undertake the training-precept to abstain from taking what is not given." According to Harvey, this also covers fraud, cheating, forgery as well as "falsely denying that one is in debt to someone".

   [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey200070-265]

3. "I undertake the training-precept to abstain from misconduct concerning sense-pleasures." This generally refers to [adultery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adultery), as well as rape and incest. It also applies to sex with those who are legally under the protection of a guardian. It is also interpreted in different ways in the varying Buddhist cultures.

   [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey200071–74-266]

4. "I undertake the training-precept to abstain from false speech." According to Harvey this includes "any form of lying, deception or exaggeration...even non-verbal deception by gesture or other indication...or misleading statements."

   [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey200075-267]

   &#x20;The precept is often also seen as including other forms of wrong speech such as "divisive speech, harsh, abusive, angry words, and even idle chatter".

   [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey200076-268]

5. "I undertake the training-precept to abstain from alcoholic drink or drugs that are an opportunity for heedlessness." According to Harvey, intoxication is seen as a way to mask rather than face the sufferings of life. It is seen as damaging to one's mental clarity, mindfulness and ability to keep the other four precepts.

   [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey200077-269]

Undertaking and upholding the five precepts is based on the principle of [non-harming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahimsa) ([Pāli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C4%81li) and [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit_language): *ahiṃsa*).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeown2013616-270] The [Pali Canon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pali_Canon) recommends one to compare oneself with others, and on the basis of that, not to hurt others.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey200033,_71-271] Compassion and a belief in [karmic retribution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karma_\(Buddhism\)) form the foundation of the precepts.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERatanakul2007241-272][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHorigan1996276-273] Undertaking the five precepts is part of regular lay devotional practice, both at home and at the local temple.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETerwiel2012178–179-274][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey200080-275] However, the extent to which people keep them differs per region and time.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELedgerwood2008152-276][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey200080-275] They are sometimes referred to as the *[śrāvakayāna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Ar%C4%81vakay%C4%81na) precepts* in the [Mahāyāna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mah%C4%81y%C4%81na) tradition, contrasting them with the [*bodhisattva* precepts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bodhisattva_Precepts).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFunayama2004105-277]

#### Vinaya

Main article: [Vinaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vinaya)



An ordination ceremony at [Wat Yannawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wat_Yannawa) in Bangkok. The Vinaya codes regulate the various sangha acts, including ordination.

Vinaya is the specific code of conduct for a *sangha* of monks or nuns. It includes the [Patimokkha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patimokkha), a set of 227 offences including 75 rules of decorum for monks, along with penalties for transgression, in the Theravadin tradition.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich1988109-278] The precise content of the *[Vinaya Pitaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vinaya_Pitaka)* (scriptures on the Vinaya) differs in different schools and tradition, and different monasteries set their own standards on its implementation. The list of *pattimokkha* is recited every fortnight in a ritual gathering of all monks.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich1988109-278] Buddhist text with vinaya rules for monasteries have been traced in all Buddhist traditions, with the oldest surviving being the ancient Chinese translations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich198893-279]

Monastic communities in the Buddhist tradition cut normal social ties to family and community and live as "islands unto themselves".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich198889–92-280] Within a monastic fraternity, a *sangha* has its own rules.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich198889–92-280] A monk abides by these institutionalised rules, and living life as the vinaya prescribes it is not merely a means, but very nearly the end in itself.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich198889–92-280] Transgressions by a monk on *Sangha* vinaya rules invites enforcement, which can include temporary or permanent expulsion.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich1988101–107-281]

### Restraint and renunciation



Living at the root of a tree (*trukkhamulik'anga*) is one of the *dhutaṅgas*, a series of optional ascetic practices for Buddhist monastics.

Another important practice taught by the Buddha is the restraint of the senses (*indriyasamvara*). In the various graduated paths, this is usually presented as a practice which is taught prior to formal sitting meditation, and which supports meditation by weakening sense desires that are a [hindrance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_hindrances) to meditation.[^cite_note-Anālayo_2003_p._71-282] According to [Anālayo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhikkhu_Analayo), sense restraint is when one "guards the sense doors in order to prevent sense impressions from leading to desires and discontent".[^cite_note-Anālayo_2003_p._71-282] This is not an avoidance of sense impression, but a kind of mindful attention towards the sense impressions which does not dwell on their main features or signs (*nimitta*). This is said to prevent harmful influences from entering the mind.[^cite_note-Anālayo_2003_p._225-283] This practice is said to give rise to an inner peace and happiness which forms a basis for concentration and insight.[^cite_note-Anālayo_2003_p._225-283]

A related Buddhist virtue and practice is renunciation, or the intent for desirelessness (*[nekkhamma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nekkhamma)*).[^cite_note-284] Generally, renunciation is the giving up of actions and desires that are seen as unwholesome on the path, such as lust for sensuality and worldly things.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERhys_DavidsStede1921–1925377"Nekkhamma"-285] Renunciation can be cultivated in different ways. The practice of giving for example, is one form of cultivating renunciation. Another one is the giving up of lay life and becoming a monastic (*bhiksu* or *bhiksuni*).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey1998199-286] Practising [celibacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celibacy) (whether for life as a monk, or temporarily) is also a form of renunciation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey200089-287] Many [Jataka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jataka_tales) stories focus on how the Buddha practised renunciation in past lives.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmmanuel2013492-288]

One way of cultivating renunciation taught by the Buddha is the contemplation (*anupassana*) of the "dangers" (or "negative consequences") of sensual pleasure (*kāmānaṃ ādīnava*). As part of the graduated discourse, this contemplation is taught after the practice of giving and morality.[^cite_note-289]

Another related practice to renunciation and sense restraint taught by the Buddha is "restraint in eating" or moderation with food, which for monks generally means not eating after noon. Devout laypersons also follow this rule during special days of religious observance (*[uposatha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uposatha)*).[^cite_note-290]

### Mindfulness and clear comprehension

The training of the faculty called ["mindfulness"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sati_\(Buddhism\)) (Pali: *sati*, Sanskrit: *smṛti,* literally meaning "recollection, remembering") is central in Buddhism. According to Analayo, mindfulness is a full awareness of the present moment which enhances and strengthens memory.[^cite_note-291] The Indian Buddhist philosopher [Asanga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asanga) defined mindfulness thus: "It is non-forgetting by the mind with regard to the object experienced. Its function is non-distraction."[^cite_note-Boin-Webb,_Sara_2001_p._9-292] According to Rupert Gethin, *sati* is also "an awareness of things in relation to things, and hence an awareness of their relative value".[^cite_note-293]

There are different practices and exercises for training mindfulness in the early discourses, such as the four *[Satipaṭṭhānas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satipatthana)* (Sanskrit: *smṛtyupasthāna*, "establishments of mindfulness") and *[Ānāpānasati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anapanasati)* (Sanskrit: *ānāpānasmṛti*, "mindfulness of breathing").

A closely related mental faculty, which is often mentioned side by side with mindfulness, is *[sampajañña](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sampaja%C3%B1%C3%B1a)* ("clear comprehension"). This faculty is the ability to comprehend what one is doing and is happening in the mind, and whether it is being influenced by unwholesome states or wholesome ones.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuan200750-294]

### Meditation

Main articles: [Buddhist meditation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_meditation), [Samadhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samadhi), [Samatha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samatha), and [Rupajhana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rupajhana)



[Kōdō Sawaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C5%8Dd%C5%8D_Sawaki) practising [Zazen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zazen) ("sitting dhyana")

A wide range of meditation practices has developed in the Buddhist traditions, but "meditation" primarily refers to the attainment of *[samādhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam%C4%81dhi_\(Buddhism\))* and the practice of *[dhyāna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhy%C4%81na_in_Buddhism)* (Pali: *jhāna*). *Samādhi* is a calm, undistracted, unified and concentrated state of awareness. It is defined by Asanga as "one-pointedness of mind on the object to be investigated. Its function consists of giving a basis to knowledge (*jñāna*)."[^cite_note-Boin-Webb,_Sara_2001_p._9-292] *Dhyāna* is "state of perfect equanimity and awareness (*upekkhā-sati-parisuddhi*)," reached through focused mental training.[^cite_note-295]

The practice of *dhyāna* aids in maintaining a calm mind and avoiding disturbance of this calm mind by mindfulness of disturbing thoughts and feelings.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams200045–46-296][^cite_note-297]

#### Origins
