The earliest evidence of yogis and their meditative tradition, states Karel Werner, is found in the [Keśin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ke%C5%9Bin) hymn 10.136 of the [Rigveda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rigveda).[^cite_note-karelwernerkesinrv-298] While evidence suggests [meditation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meditation) was practised in the centuries preceding the Buddha,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarrithers198630-299] the meditative methodologies described in the Buddhist texts are some of the earliest among texts that have survived into the modern era.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich198844-300][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller19968-301] These methodologies likely incorporate what existed before the Buddha as well as those first developed within Buddhism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBronkhorst19931–17-302][^cite_note-306]

There is no scholarly agreement on the origin and source of the practice of *dhyāna.* Some scholars, like Bronkhorst, see the *four dhyānas* as a Buddhist invention.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBronkhorst199399-307] Alexander Wynne argues that the Buddha learned *dhyāna* from Brahmanical teachers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWynne2007\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_October_2020\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (October_2020)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-308]

Whatever the case, the Buddha taught meditation with a new focus and interpretation, particularly through the *four dhyānas* methodology,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBronkhorst1993Part_I:_page_5-309] in which mindfulness is maintained.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBronkhorst199388-310][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich2007-311] Further, the focus of meditation and the underlying theory of liberation guiding the meditation has been different in Buddhism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarrithers198630-299][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENorman199729-312][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich1997131-313] For example, states Bronkhorst, the verse 4.4.23 of the *Brihadaranyaka Upanishad* with its "become calm, subdued, quiet, patiently enduring, concentrated, one sees soul in oneself" is most probably a meditative state.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBronkhorst1993Chapter_9,_page_86-314] The Buddhist discussion of meditation is without the concept of soul and the discussion criticises both the ascetic meditation of Jainism and the "real self, soul" meditation of Hinduism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBronkhorst199374_(Chapter_8),_102_(Conclusion)-315]

#### The formless attainments

Often grouped into the *jhāna*-scheme are four other meditative states, referred to in the early texts as *arupa samāpattis* (formless attainments). These are also referred to in commentarial literature as immaterial/formless *jhānas* (*arūpajhānas*). The first formless attainment is a place or realm of infinite space (*ākāsānañcāyatana*) without form or colour or shape. The second is termed the realm of infinite consciousness (*viññāṇañcāyatana*); the third is the realm of nothingness (*ākiñcaññāyatana*), while the fourth is the realm of "neither perception nor non-perception".[^cite_note-Wayman1984p86-316] The four *rupa-jhānas* in Buddhist practice leads to rebirth in successfully better *rupa* Brahma heavenly realms, while *arupa-jhānas* leads into arupa heavens.[^cite_note-317][^cite_note-318]

#### Meditation and insight

See also: [Meditation and insight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Noble_Truths#Substituting_%22liberating_insight%22) and [Yoga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoga)

In the Pali Canon, the Buddha outlines two meditative qualities which are mutually supportive: *[samatha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samatha)* (Pāli; Sanskrit: *śamatha*; "calm") and *[vipassanā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vipassan%C4%81)* (Sanskrit: *vipaśyanā*, insight).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBodhi2005269–270,_440_''n''._13-319] The Buddha compares these mental qualities to a "swift pair of messengers" who together help deliver the message of *nibbana* (SN 35.245).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBodhi20001251–1253-320]

The various Buddhist traditions generally see Buddhist meditation as being divided into those two main types.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelch1967396-321][^cite_note-322] Samatha is also called "calming meditation", and focuses on stilling and concentrating the mind i.e. developing samadhi and the four *dhyānas*. According to [Damien Keown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damien_Keown), *vipassanā* meanwhile, focuses on "the generation of penetrating and critical insight (*paññā*)".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeown1996106–107,_context:_Chapter_7-323]

There are numerous doctrinal positions and disagreements within the different Buddhist traditions regarding these qualities or forms of meditation. For example, in the Pali *Four Ways to Arahantship Sutta* (AN 4.170), it is said that one can develop calm and then insight, or insight and then calm, or both at the same time.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBodhi2005268,_439-324] Meanwhile, in Vasubandhu's *Abhidharmakośakārikā*, vipaśyanā is said to be practised once one has reached samadhi by cultivating the four foundations of mindfulness (*smṛtyupasthāna*s).[^cite_note-325]

Beginning with comments by [La Vallee Poussin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_de_La_Vall%C3%A9e-Poussin), a series of scholars have argued that these two meditation types reflect a tension between two different ancient Buddhist traditions regarding the use of *dhyāna,* one which focused on insight based practice and the other which focused purely on *dhyāna*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBronkhorst1993-326][^cite_note-buddhismuskunde.uni-hamburg.de-327] However, other scholars such as Analayo and Rupert Gethin have disagreed with this "two paths" thesis, instead seeing both of these practices as complementary.[^cite_note-buddhismuskunde.uni-hamburg.de-327][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGethin2001xiv-328]

#### The *Brahma-vihara*

Main article: [Brahmavihara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahmavihara)

The four immeasurables or four abodes, also called *Brahma-viharas*, are virtues or directions for meditation in Buddhist traditions, which helps a person be reborn in the heavenly (Brahma) realm.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHirakawa1993172–174-329][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2013154,_326-330][^cite_note-331] These are traditionally believed to be a characteristic of the deity Brahma and the heavenly abode he resides in.[^cite_note-332]

The four *Brahma-vihara* are:

1. Loving-kindness (Pāli: *[mettā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mett%C4%81)*, Sanskrit: *maitrī*) is active good will towards all;

   [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2013154,_326-330]

   [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFowler199960–62-333]

2. Compassion (Pāli and Sanskrit: *[karuṇā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karu%E1%B9%87%C4%81)*) results from *metta*; it is identifying the suffering of others as one's own;

   [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2013154,_326-330]

   [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFowler199960–62-333]

3. Empathetic joy (Pāli and Sanskrit: *[muditā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mudita)*): is the feeling of joy because others are happy, even if one did not contribute to it; it is a form of sympathetic joy;

   [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFowler199960–62-333]

4. Equanimity (Pāli: *[upekkhā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upekkha)*, Sanskrit: *upekṣā*): is even-mindedness and serenity, treating everyone impartially.

   [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2013154,_326-330]

   [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFowler199960–62-333]

#### Tantra, visualisation and the subtle body

See also: [Tibetan Tantric Practice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibetan_Tantric_Practice) and [Vajrayana § Tantra_techniques](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vajrayana#Tantra_techniques)



An 18th century Mongolian miniature which depicts the generation of the Vairocana Mandala

Some Buddhist traditions, especially those associated with Tantric Buddhism (also known as Vajrayana and Secret Mantra) use images and symbols of deities and Buddhas in meditation. This is generally done by mentally visualising a Buddha image (or some other mental image, like a symbol, a mandala, a syllable, etc.), and using that image to cultivate calm and insight. One may also visualise and identify oneself with the imagined deity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETrainor200487-334][^cite_note-335] While visualisation practices have been particularly popular in Vajrayana, they may also found in Mahayana and Theravada traditions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETrainor200486–87-336]

In Tibetan Buddhism, unique tantric techniques which include visualisation (but also [mantra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mantra) recitation, [mandalas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandala), and other elements) are considered to be much more effective than non-tantric meditations and they are one of the most popular meditation methods.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPowers2007250-337] The methods of *[Unsurpassable Yoga Tantra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anuttarayoga_Tantra)*, (*anuttarayogatantra*) are in turn seen as the highest and most advanced. Anuttarayoga practice is divided into two stages, the *Generation Stage* and the *Completion Stage.* In the Generation Stage, one meditates on emptiness and visualises oneself as a deity as well as visualising its mandala. The focus is on developing clear appearance and divine pride (the understanding that oneself and the deity are one).[^cite_note-338] This method is also known as deity yoga (*devata yoga*). There are numerous meditation deities (*[yidam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yidam)*) used, each with a mandala, a circular symbolic map used in meditation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETrainor200488–89-339]

### Insight and knowledge

Main articles: [Prajñā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Praj%C3%B1%C4%81_\(Buddhism\)), [Bodhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bodhi), [Kenshō](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kensh%C5%8D), [Satori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satori), [Subitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subitism), and [Vipassana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vipassana)

*Prajñā* (Sanskrit) or *paññā* (Pāli) is [wisdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisdom), or knowledge of the true nature of existence. Another term which is associated with *prajñā* and sometimes is equivalent to it is *vipassanā* (Pāli) or *vipaśyanā* (Sanskrit), which is often translated as "insight". In Buddhist texts, the faculty of insight is often said to be cultivated through the four establishments of mindfulness.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuan200758-340] In the early texts, *Paññā* is included as one of the "five faculties" (*[indriya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indriya)*) which are commonly listed as important spiritual elements to be cultivated (see for example: AN I 16). *Paññā* along with samadhi, is also listed as one of the "trainings in the higher states of mind" (*adhicittasikkha*).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuan200758-340]

The Buddhist tradition regards ignorance ([*avidyā*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avidy%C4%81_\(Buddhism\))), a fundamental ignorance, misunderstanding or mis-perception of the nature of reality, as one of the basic causes of *dukkha* and *samsara*. Overcoming this ignorance is part of the path to awakening. This overcoming includes the contemplation of impermanence and the non-self nature of reality,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETrainor200474-341][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConze201339–40-342] and this develops dispassion for the objects of [clinging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Up%C4%81d%C4%81na), and liberates a being from *dukkha* and *saṃsāra*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFowler199949–52-343][^cite_note-344][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGethin199873–75,_146–159,_243-345]

*Prajñā* is important in all Buddhist traditions. It is variously described as wisdom regarding the impermanent and [not-self](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatt%C4%81) nature of dharmas (phenomena), the functioning of karma and rebirth, and knowledge of dependent origination.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBuswell2004664–665-346] Likewise, *vipaśyanā* is described in a similar way, such as in the *[Paṭisambhidāmagga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pa%E1%B9%ADisambhid%C4%81magga)*, where it is said to be the contemplation of things as impermanent, unsatisfactory and [not-self](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatt%C4%81).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuan200759-347]

### Vegetarianism and animal ethics

Main article: [Buddhist vegetarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_vegetarianism)

Based on the Indian principle of [ahimsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahi%E1%B9%83s%C4%81) (non-harming), the Buddha's ethics strongly condemn the harming of all sentient beings, including all animals. He thus condemned the animal sacrifice of the Brahmins as well as hunting, and killing animals for food.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2000157–158-348] However, early Buddhist texts depict the Buddha as allowing monastics to eat meat. This seems to be because monastics begged for their food and thus were supposed to accept whatever food was offered to them.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2000156–159-349] This was tempered by the rule that meat had to be "three times clean": "they had not seen, had not heard, and had no reason to suspect that the animal had been killed so that the meat could be given to them".[^cite_note-Phelps,_Norm_2004_p._76-350] Also, while the Buddha did not explicitly promote vegetarianism in his discourses, he did state that gaining one's livelihood from the meat trade was unethical.[^cite_note-351] In contrast to this, various Mahayana sutras and texts like the [Mahaparinirvana sutra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mah%C4%81y%C4%81na_Mah%C4%81parinirv%C4%81%E1%B9%87a_S%C5%ABtra), [Surangama sutra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9A%C5%ABra%E1%B9%85gama_S%C5%ABtra) and the [Lankavatara sutra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La%E1%B9%85k%C4%81vat%C4%81ra_S%C5%ABtra) state that the Buddha promoted vegetarianism out of compassion.[^cite_note-352] Indian Mahayana thinkers like Shantideva promoted the avoidance of meat.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2000163-353] Throughout history, the issue of whether Buddhists should be vegetarian has remained a much debated topic and there is a variety of opinions on this issue among modern Buddhists.

## Texts

Main article: [Buddhist texts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_texts)



A depiction of the supposed [First Buddhist council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Buddhist_council) at [Rajgir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajgir). Communal recitation was one of the original ways of transmitting and preserving Early Buddhist texts.

Buddhism, like all Indian religions, was initially an [oral tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oral_tradition) in ancient times.[^cite_note-lopez2004busc1-354] The Buddha's words, the early doctrines, concepts, and their traditional interpretations were orally transmitted from one generation to the next. The earliest oral texts were transmitted in Middle [Indo-Aryan languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Aryan_languages) called [Prakrits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prakrits), such as [Pali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pali), through the use of communal recitation and other [mnemonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mnemonic) techniques.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGethin199839–41-355] The first Buddhist canonical texts were likely written down in Sri Lanka, about 400 years after the Buddha died.[^cite_note-lopez2004busc1-354] The texts were part of the *[Tripitakas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tripi%E1%B9%ADaka)*, and many versions appeared thereafter claiming to be the words of the Buddha. Scholarly Buddhist commentary texts, with named authors, appeared in India, around the 2nd century CE.[^cite_note-lopez2004busc1-354] These texts were written in Pali or Sanskrit, sometimes regional languages, as [palm-leaf manuscripts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palm-leaf_manuscript), birch bark, painted scrolls, carved into temple walls, and later on paper.[^cite_note-lopez2004busc1-354]

Unlike what the [Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bible) is to Christianity and the [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran) is to Islam, but like all major ancient Indian religions, there is no consensus among the different Buddhist traditions as to what constitutes the scriptures or a common canon in Buddhism.[^cite_note-lopez2004busc1-354] The general belief among Buddhists is that the canonical corpus is vast.[^cite_note-356][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGethin2008xiv-357][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEliot193516-358] This corpus includes the ancient *Sutras* organised into *[Nikayas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nik%C4%81ya)* or *[Agamas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%80gama_\(Buddhism\))*, itself the part of three basket of texts called the *Tripitakas*.[^cite_note-359] Each Buddhist tradition has its own collection of texts, much of which is translation of ancient Pali and Sanskrit Buddhist texts of India. The [Chinese Buddhist canon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Buddhist_canon), for example, includes 2184 texts in 55 volumes, while the [Tibetan canon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibetan_Buddhist_canon) comprises 1108 texts – all claimed to have been spoken by the Buddha – and another 3461 texts composed by Indian scholars revered in the Tibetan tradition.[^cite_note-lopez2004busc2-360] The Buddhist textual history is vast; over 40,000 manuscripts – mostly Buddhist, some non-Buddhist – were discovered in 1900 in the Dunhuang Chinese cave alone.[^cite_note-lopez2004busc2-360]

### Early texts

Main article: [Early Buddhist Texts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Buddhist_Texts)



Gandhara birchbark scroll fragments (c. 1st century) from British Library Collection

The Early Buddhist Texts refers to the literature which is considered by modern scholars to be the earliest Buddhist material. The first four [Pali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pali) [Nikayas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikayas), and the corresponding Chinese [Āgamas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%80gama_\(Buddhism\)) are generally considered to be among the earliest material.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGethin199842–43-361][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESujatoBrahmali20159–10-362][^cite_note-363] Apart from these, there are also fragmentary collections of EBT materials in other languages such as [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit), [Khotanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saka_language), [Tibetan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Tibetan) and [Gāndhārī](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C4%81ndh%C4%81r%C4%AB_language). The modern study of [early Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-sectarian_Buddhism) often relies on comparative scholarship using these various early Buddhist sources to identify parallel texts and common doctrinal content.[^cite_note-364] One feature of these early texts are literary structures which reflect oral transmission, such as widespread repetition.[^cite_note-365]

### The Tripitakas

Main articles: [Tripiṭaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tripi%E1%B9%ADaka) and [Pali Canon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pali_Canon)

After the development of the different [early Buddhist schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Buddhist_schools), these schools began to develop their own textual collections, which were termed *Tripiṭakas* (Triple Baskets).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWarder2000282–283-366]

Many early *Tripiṭakas*, like the Pāli *Tipitaka*, were divided into three sections: *[Vinaya Pitaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vinaya)* (focuses on [monastic rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monasticism)), *[Sutta Pitaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sutta_Pitaka)* (Buddhist discourses) and *[Abhidhamma Pitaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abhidhamma),* which contain expositions and commentaries on the doctrine. The [Pāli *Tipitaka*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C4%81li_Canon) (also known as the Pali Canon) of the Theravada School constitutes the only complete collection of Buddhist texts in an [Indic language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Aryan_languages) which has survived until today.[^cite_note-367] However, many *Sutras*, *Vinayas* and *Abhidharma* works from other schools survive in Chinese translation, as part of the Chinese Buddhist Canon. According to some sources, some early schools of Buddhism had five or seven *pitakas*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESkilling1992114-368]

### Mahāyāna texts

Main article: [Mahayana sutras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahayana_sutras)

Tripiṭaka Koreana in South Korea, over 81,000 wood printing blocks stored in racks

The [Tripiṭaka Koreana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tripitaka_Koreana) in South Korea, an edition of the [Chinese Buddhist canon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Buddhist_canon) carved and preserved in over 81,000 wood printing blocks

The [Mahāyāna sūtras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahayana_sutras) are a very broad genre of Buddhist scriptures that the [Mahāyāna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahayana) Buddhist tradition holds are original teachings of [the Buddha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gautama_Buddha). Modern historians generally hold that the first of these texts were composed probably around the 1st century BCE or 1st century CE.[^cite_note-Buddhism_2004,_page_293-369][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHirakawa1993252-370][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBuswell2004494-371] In Mahāyāna, these texts are generally given greater authority than the early Āgamas and Abhidharma literature, which are called "[Śrāvakayāna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Ar%C4%81vakay%C4%81na)" or "[Hinayana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinayana)" to distinguish them from Mahāyāna sūtras.[^cite_note-372] Mahāyāna traditions mainly see these different classes of texts as being designed for different types of persons, with different levels of spiritual understanding. The Mahāyāna sūtras are mainly seen as being for those of "greater" capacity.[^cite_note-373] Mahāyāna also has a very large literature of philosophical and exegetical texts. These are often called [*śāstra*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shastras) (treatises) or *vrittis* (commentaries). Some of this literature was also written in verse form (*karikās*), the most famous of which is the *[Mūlamadhyamika-karikā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mulamadhyamakakarika)* (Root Verses on the Middle Way) by [Nagarjuna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nagarjuna), the foundational text of the [Madhyamika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madhyamika) school.

### Tantric texts

Main article: [Tantras (Buddhism)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tantras_\(Buddhism\))

During the [Gupta Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gupta_Empire), a new class of Buddhist sacred literature began to develop, which are called the [Tantras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tantras_\(Buddhism\)).[^cite_note-374] By the 8th century, the tantric tradition was very influential in India and beyond. Besides drawing on a [Mahāyāna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mah%C4%81y%C4%81na) Buddhist framework, these texts also borrowed deities and material from other Indian religious traditions, such as the [Śaiva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Aaiva) and [Pancharatra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pancharatra) traditions, local god/goddess cults, and local spirit worship (such as [yaksha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yaksha) or [nāga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N%C4%81ga) spirits).[^cite_note-375][^cite_note-376]

Some features of these texts include the widespread use of mantras, meditation on the [subtle body](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lung_\(Tibetan_Buddhism\)#Subtle_Body), worship of [fierce deities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fierce_deities), and [antinomian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antinomian) and [transgressive](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/transgressive) practices such as ingesting [alcohol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcohol_\(drug\)) and performing sexual rituals.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliamsTribeWynne2012chapter_7-377][^cite_note-378][^cite_note-379]

## History

Main article: [History of Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Buddhism)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Buddhism).

### Historical roots

Historically, the roots of Buddhism lie in the religious thought of [Iron Age India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Age_India) around the middle of the first millennium BCE.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGethin2008xv-380] This was a period of great intellectual ferment and socio-cultural change known as the ["Second urbanisation"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_India#Second_urbanisation_\(c._600_%E2%80%93_200_BCE\)), marked by the growth of towns and trade, the composition of the [Upanishads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upanishads) and the historical emergence of the [Śramaṇa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Arama%E1%B9%87a) traditions.[^cite_note-381][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich198826–41-382][^cite_note-385]

New ideas developed both in the [Vedic tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_Vedic_religion) in the form of the Upanishads, and outside of the Vedic tradition through the Śramaṇa movements.[^cite_note-386][^cite_note-387][^cite_note-Olivelle1992-388] The term Śramaṇa refers to several Indian religious movements parallel to but separate from the [historical Vedic religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_Vedic_religion), including Buddhism, [Jainism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jainism) and others such as [Ājīvika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%80j%C4%ABvika).[^cite_note-389]

Several Śramaṇa movements are known to have existed in India before the 6th century BCE (pre-Buddha, pre-[Mahavira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahavira)), and these influenced both the [āstika and nāstika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%80stika_and_n%C4%81stika) traditions of [Indian philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_philosophy).[^cite_note-reginaldray247-390] According to Martin Wiltshire, the Śramaṇa tradition evolved in India over two phases, namely [Paccekabuddha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pratyekabuddha) and [Savaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Ar%C4%81vaka) phases, the former being the tradition of individual ascetic and the latter of disciples, and that Buddhism and Jainism ultimately emerged from these.[^cite_note-wiltshire293-391] [Brahmanical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_Vedic_religion) and non-Brahmanical ascetic groups shared and used several similar ideas,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESamuel2010123–125-392] but the Śramaṇa traditions also drew upon already established Brahmanical concepts and philosophical roots, states Wiltshire, to formulate their own doctrines.[^cite_note-reginaldray247-390][^cite_note-wiltshire226-393] Brahmanical motifs can be found in the oldest Buddhist texts, using them to introduce and explain Buddhist ideas.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShults2014126-394] For example, prior to Buddhist developments, the Brahmanical tradition internalised and variously reinterpreted the [three Vedic sacrificial fires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Srauta) as concepts such as Truth, Rite, Tranquility or Restraint.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShults2014127-395] Buddhist texts also refer to the three Vedic sacrificial fires, reinterpreting and explaining them as ethical conduct.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShults2014125–129-396]

The Śramaṇa religions challenged and broke with the Brahmanic tradition on core assumptions such as [Atman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atman_\(Hinduism\)) (soul, self), [Brahman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahman), the nature of afterlife, and they rejected the authority of the [Vedas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vedas) and [Upanishads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upanishad).[^cite_note-397][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJaini200147–48-398][^cite_note-Siderits2007p16-399] Buddhism was one among several Indian religions that did so.[^cite_note-Siderits2007p16-399]

Early Buddhist positions in the [Theravada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theravada) tradition had not established any deities, but were epistemologically cautious rather than directly [atheist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheist). Later Buddhist traditions were more influenced by the critique of deities within Hinduism and therefore more committed to a strongly atheist stance. These developments were historic and epistemological as documented in verses from [Śāntideva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9A%C4%81ntideva)'s [Bodhicaryāvatāra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bodhicary%C4%81vat%C4%81ra), and supplemented by reference to [suttas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suttas) and [jātakas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jataka_tales) from the [Pali Canon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pali_Canon).[^cite_note-400]

### Indian Buddhism

Main article: [History of Buddhism in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Buddhism_in_India)



[Ajanta Caves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ajanta_Caves), Cave 10, a first period type [chaitya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaitya) worship hall with [stupa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stupa) but no idols

The history of Indian Buddhism may be divided into five periods:[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHirakawa19937-401] Early Buddhism (occasionally called [pre-sectarian Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-sectarian_Buddhism)), [Nikaya Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikaya_Buddhism) or Sectarian Buddhism (the period of the early Buddhist schools), Early [Mahayana Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahayana), Late Mahayana, and the era of [Vajrayana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vajrayana) or the "Tantric Age".

#### Pre-sectarian Buddhism

Main article: [Pre-sectarian Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-sectarian_Buddhism)

According to [Lambert Schmithausen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lambert_Schmithausen) Pre-sectarian Buddhism is "the canonical period prior to the development of different schools with their different positions".[^cite_note-402]

The [early Buddhist Texts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Buddhist_Texts) include the four principal Pali [Nikāyas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nik%C4%81ya)[^cite_note-403] (and their parallel [Agamas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%80gama_\(Buddhism\)) found in the Chinese canon) together with the main body of monastic rules, which survive in the various versions of the [patimokkha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patimokkha).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESujatoBrahmali201539–41-404][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGethin2008xviii-405][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey19983-406] However, these texts were revised over time, and it is unclear what constitutes the earliest layer of Buddhist teachings. One method to obtain information on the oldest core of Buddhism is to compare the oldest extant versions of the Theravadin Pāli Canon and other texts.[^cite_note-409] The reliability of the early sources, and the possibility to draw out a core of oldest teachings, is a matter of dispute.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVetter1988xxi–xxxvii-410] According to Vetter, inconsistencies remain, and other methods must be applied to resolve those inconsistencies.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVetter1988ix-407][^cite_note-414]

According to Schmithausen, three positions held by scholars of Buddhism can be distinguished:[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBronkhorst1993vii-415]

1. "Stress on the fundamental homogeneity and substantial authenticity of at least a considerable part of the Nikayic materials". Proponents of this position include [A. K. Warder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A._K._Warder)

   [\[note 22\]](#cite_note-417)

   &#x20;and [Richard Gombrich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Gombrich).

   [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBronkhorst1993viii-418]

   [\[note 23\]](#cite_note-419)

2. "Scepticism with regard to the possibility of retrieving the doctrine of earliest Buddhism". Ronald Davidson is a proponent of this position.

   [\[note 24\]](#cite_note-421)

3. "Cautious optimism in this respect". Proponents of this position include J.W. de Jong,

   [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJong199325-422]

   [\[note 25\]](#cite_note-Jong-423)

   &#x20;Johannes Bronkhorst

   [\[note 26\]](#cite_note-424)

   &#x20;and Donald Lopez.

   [\[note 27\]](#cite_note-426)

##### The Core teachings

According to Mitchell, certain basic teachings appear in many places throughout the early texts, which has led most scholars to conclude that Gautama Buddha must have taught something similar to the [Four Noble Truths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Noble_Truths), the [Noble Eightfold Path](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noble_Eightfold_Path), [Nirvana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nirvana_\(Buddhism\)), the [three marks of existence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_marks_of_existence), the [five aggregates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skandha), [dependent origination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prat%C4%ABtyasamutp%C4%81da), [karma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karma_in_Buddhism) and [rebirth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebirth_\(Buddhism\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMitchell200234-427]

According to N. Ross Reat, all of these doctrines are shared by the Theravada Pali texts and the Mahasamghika school's *[Śālistamba Sūtra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salistamba_Sutra)*.[^cite_note-428] A recent study by Bhikkhu Analayo concludes that the Theravada *[Majjhima Nikaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majjhima_Nik%C4%81ya)* and Sarvastivada *[Madhyama Agama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madhyama_Agama)* contain mostly the same major doctrines.[^cite_note-429] [Richard Salomon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_G._Salomon_\(professor_of_Asian_studies\)), in his study of the Gandharan texts (which are the earliest manuscripts containing early discourses), has confirmed that their teachings are "consistent with non-Mahayana Buddhism, which survives today in the Theravada school of Sri Lanka and Southeast Asia, but which in ancient times was represented by eighteen separate schools."[^cite_note-430]

However, some scholars argue that critical analysis reveals discrepancies among the various doctrines found in these early texts, which point to alternative possibilities for early Buddhism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESkorupski19905-431][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBronkhorst19984,_11-432][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchopen2002-433] The authenticity of certain teachings and doctrines have been questioned. For example, some scholars think that karma was not central to the teaching of the historical Buddha, while other disagree with this position.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMatthews1986124-434][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBronkhorst199814-435] Likewise, there is scholarly disagreement on whether insight was seen as liberating in early Buddhism or whether it was a later addition to the practice of the four *jhānas*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchmithausen1981-411][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBronkhorst199377–78,_Section_8.4.3-436][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVetter19885,_Quote:_\[T\]hey_do_not_teach_that_one_is_released_by_knowing_the_four_noble_truths,_but_by_practising_the_fourth_noble_truth,_the_eightfold_path,_which_culminates_in_right_samadhi-437] Scholars such as Bronkhorst also think that the four noble truths may not have been formulated in earliest Buddhism, and did not serve in earliest Buddhism as a description of "liberating insight".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBronkhorst1993107-438] According to Vetter, the description of the Buddhist path may initially have been as simple as the term "the middle way".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVetter1988-157] In time, this short description was elaborated, resulting in the description of the eightfold path.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVetter1988-157]

#### Ashokan Era and the early schools

Main articles: [Early Buddhist schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Buddhist_schools), [Buddhist councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_councils), and [Theravada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theravada)

According to numerous Buddhist scriptures, soon after the [*parinirvāṇa*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parinirvana) (from Sanskrit: "highest extinguishment") of Gautama Buddha, the [first Buddhist council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Buddhist_council) was held to collectively recite the teachings to ensure that no errors occurred in oral transmission. Many modern scholars question the historicity of this event.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201388–90-439] However, [Richard Gombrich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Gombrich) states that the monastic assembly recitations of the Buddha's teaching likely began during Buddha's lifetime, and they served a similar role of codifying the teachings.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams2005175–176-440]

The so called [Second Buddhist council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Buddhist_council) resulted in the first schism in the [Sangha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sangha). Modern scholars believe that this was probably caused when a group of reformists called [Sthaviras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sthavira_Nik%C4%81ya) ("elders") sought to modify the Vinaya (monastic rule), and this caused a split with the conservatives who rejected this change, they were called [Mahāsāṃghikas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mah%C4%81s%C4%81%E1%B9%83ghika).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201389–90-441][^cite_note-442] While most scholars accept that this happened at some point, there is no agreement on the dating, especially if it dates to before or after the reign of Ashoka.[^cite_note-443]



Map of the Buddhist missions during the reign of [Ashoka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashoka) according to the Edicts of Ashoka

Buddhism may have spread only slowly throughout India until the time of the [Mauryan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurya_Empire) emperor [Ashoka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashoka) (304–232 BCE), who was a public supporter of the religion. The support of Aśoka and his descendants led to the construction of more [stūpas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stupa) (such as at [Sanchi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanchi) and [Bharhut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bharhut)), temples (such as the [Mahabodhi Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahabodhi_Temple)) and to its spread throughout the Maurya Empire and into neighbouring lands such as [Central Asia](6742.md) and to the island of Sri Lanka.

During and after the Mauryan period (322–180 BCE), the Sthavira community gave rise to several schools, one of which was the [Theravada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theravada) school which tended to congregate in the south and another which was the [Sarvāstivāda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarv%C4%81stiv%C4%81da) school, which was mainly in north India. Likewise, the [Mahāsāṃghika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mah%C4%81s%C4%81%E1%B9%83ghika) groups also eventually split into different Sanghas. Originally, these schisms were caused by disputes over monastic disciplinary codes of various fraternities, but eventually, by about 100 CE if not earlier, schisms were being caused by doctrinal disagreements too.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey199874–75-444]

Following (or leading up to) the schisms, each Saṅgha started to accumulate their own version of [Tripiṭaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tripi%E1%B9%ADaka) (triple basket of texts).[^cite_note-britannicatipitaka-71][^cite_note-Crandall2012p56-445] In their Tripiṭaka, each school included the Suttas of the Buddha, a Vinaya basket (disciplinary code) and some schools also added an [Abhidharma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abhidharma) basket which were texts on detailed scholastic classification, summary and interpretation of the Suttas.[^cite_note-britannicatipitaka-71][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201390–91-446] The doctrine details in the Abhidharmas of various Buddhist schools differ significantly, and these were composed starting about the third century BCE and through the 1st millennium CE.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201390–93-447][^cite_note-448][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeownPrebish2004485-449]

### Post-Ashokan expansion

Main article: [Silk Road transmission of Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silk_Road_transmission_of_Buddhism)

According to the [edicts of Aśoka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edicts_of_Ashoka), the Mauryan emperor sent emissaries to various countries west of India to spread "Dharma", particularly in eastern provinces of the neighbouring [Seleucid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seleucid_Empire), and even farther to [Hellenistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic_civilization) kingdoms of the Mediterranean. It is a matter of disagreement among scholars whether or not these emissaries were accompanied by Buddhist missionaries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich2005a135-450]



Buddhist expansion throughout Asia

In central and west Asia, Buddhist influence grew, through Greek-speaking Buddhist monarchs and ancient Asian trade routes, a phenomenon known as [Greco-Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Buddhism). An example of this is evidenced in Chinese and Pali Buddhist records, such as *[Milindapanha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milinda_Panha)* and the [Greco-Buddhist art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Buddhist_art) of [Gandhāra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gandhara). The *Milindapanha* describes a conversation between a Buddhist monk and the 2nd-century BCE Greek king [Menander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menander_I), after which Menander abdicates and himself goes into monastic life in the pursuit of nirvana.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETrainor2004103,_119-451][^cite_note-452] Some scholars have questioned the *Milindapanha* version, expressing doubts whether Menander was Buddhist or just favourably disposed to Buddhist monks.[^cite_note-453]

The [Kushan Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kushan_Empire) (30–375 CE) came to control the Silk Road trade through Central and South Asia, which brought them to interact with [Gandharan Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gandharan_Buddhism) and the Buddhist institutions of these regions. The Kushans patronised Buddhism throughout their lands, and many Buddhist centres were built or renovated (the Sarvastivada school was particularly favored), especially by Emperor Kanishka (128–151 CE).[^cite_note-454][^cite_note-Heirman,_Ann_p._57-455] Kushan support helped Buddhism to expand into a world religion through their trade routes.[^cite_note-Liu2010p42-456] Buddhism spread to [Khotan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Khotan), the [Tarim Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarim_Basin), and China, eventually to other parts of the far east.[^cite_note-Heirman,_Ann_p._57-455] Some of the earliest written documents of the Buddhist faith are the [Gandharan Buddhist texts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gandharan_Buddhist_texts), dating from about the 1st century CE, and connected to the [Dharmaguptaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharmaguptaka) school.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWarder2000278-457][^cite_note-458][^cite_note-459]

The [Islamic conquest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquest_of_Persia) of the [Iranian Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Plateau) in the 7th-century, followed by the [Muslim conquests of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquests_of_Afghanistan) and the later establishment of the [Ghaznavid kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghaznavids) with Islam as the state religion in Central Asia between the 10th- and 12th-century led to the decline and disappearance of Buddhism from most of these regions.[^cite_note-Kudara2002-460]

### Mahāyāna Buddhism

Main article: [Mahāyāna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mah%C4%81y%C4%81na)

stone statue group, a Buddhist triad depicting, left to right, a Kushan, the future buddha Maitreya, Gautama Buddha, the bodhisattva Avalokiteśvara, and a Buddhist monk. 2nd–3rd century. Guimet Museum

A Buddhist triad depicting, left to right, a [Kushan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kushan_Empire), the future buddha [Maitreya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maitreya), [Gautama Buddha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gautama_Buddha), the bodhisattva [Avalokiteśvara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avalokite%C5%9Bvara), and a [monk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhikkhu). Second–third century. [Guimet Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guimet_Museum).

The origins of Mahāyāna ("Great Vehicle") Buddhism are not well understood and there are various competing theories about how and where this movement arose. Theories include the idea that it began as various groups venerating certain texts or that it arose as a strict forest ascetic movement.[^cite_note-Drewes,_David_2010-461]

The first Mahāyāna works were written sometime between the 1st century BCE and the 2nd century CE.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHirakawa1993252-370][^cite_note-Drewes,_David_2010-461] Much of the early extant evidence for the origins of Mahāyāna comes from early Chinese translations of Mahāyāna texts, mainly those of [Lokakṣema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lokaksema_\(Buddhist_monk\)). (2nd century CE).[^cite_note-China_Buswell_2004-463] Some scholars have traditionally considered the earliest [Mahāyāna sūtras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahayana_sutras) to include the first versions of the [Prajnaparamita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prajnaparamita) series, along with texts concerning [Akṣobhya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akshobhya), which were probably composed in the 1st century BCE in the south of India.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHirakawa1993252–253,_263,_268-464][^cite_note-South-466]

There is no evidence that Mahāyāna ever referred to a separate formal school or sect of Buddhism, with a separate monastic code (Vinaya), but rather that it existed as a certain set of ideals, and later doctrines, for bodhisattvas.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENattier2003193–194-467][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams20084–5-468] Records written by Chinese monks visiting India indicate that both Mahāyāna and non-Mahāyāna monks could be found in the same monasteries, with the difference that Mahāyāna monks worshipped figures of Bodhisattvas, while non-Mahayana monks did not.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams200097-469]



Site of [Nalanda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nalanda) University, a great centre of Mahāyāna thought

Mahāyāna initially seems to have remained a small minority movement that was in tension with other Buddhist groups, struggling for wider acceptance.[^cite_note-470] However, during the fifth and sixth centuries CE, there seems to have been a rapid growth of Mahāyāna Buddhism, which is shown by a large increase in epigraphic and manuscript evidence in this period. However, it still remained a minority in comparison to other Buddhist schools.[^cite_note-471]

Mahāyāna Buddhist institutions continued to grow in influence during the following centuries, with large monastic university complexes such as [Nalanda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nalanda) (established by the 5th-century CE Gupta emperor, [Kumaragupta I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kumaragupta_I)) and [Vikramashila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vikramashila) (established under [Dharmapala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharmapala_\(emperor\)) c. 783 to 820) becoming quite powerful and influential. During this period of Late Mahāyāna, four major types of thought developed: Mādhyamaka, Yogācāra, Buddha-nature (*Tathāgatagarbha*), and the [epistemological tradition of Dignaga and Dharmakirti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_logico-epistemology#The_Dign%C4%81ga-Dharmak%C4%ABrti_tradition).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHirakawa19938–9-472] According to [Dan Lusthaus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dan_Lusthaus), Mādhyamaka and Yogācāra have a great deal in common, and the commonality stems from early Buddhism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELusthaus2002236–237-473]

### Late Indian Buddhism and Tantra

Main article: [Vajrayana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vajrayana)



Vajrayana adopted deities such as [Bhairava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhairava), known as [Yamantaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yamantaka) in Tibetan Buddhism.

During the [Gupta period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gupta_Empire) (4th–6th centuries) and the empire of [Harṣavardana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harsha) (c. 590–647 CE), Buddhism continued to be influential in India, and large Buddhist learning institutions such as [Nalanda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nalanda) and [Valabahi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valabhi_University) Universities were at their peak.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWarder2000442-474] Buddhism also flourished under the support of the [Pāla Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pala_Empire) (8th–12th centuries). Under the Guptas and Palas, Tantric Buddhism or Vajrayana developed and rose to prominence. It promoted new practices such as the use of [mantras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mantra), [dharanis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharani), [mudras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mudra), [mandalas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandala) and the visualisation of deities and Buddhas and developed a new class of literature, the [Buddhist Tantras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tantras_\(Buddhism\)). This new esoteric form of Buddhism can be traced back to groups of wandering yogi magicians called [mahasiddhas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahasiddha).[^cite_note-475][^cite_note-476]

The question of the origins of early Vajrayana has been taken up by various scholars. [David Seyfort Ruegg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Seyfort_Ruegg) has suggested that Buddhist tantra employed various elements of a "pan-Indian religious substrate" which is not specifically Buddhist, Shaiva or Vaishnava.[^cite_note-477]

According to Indologist [Alexis Sanderson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexis_Sanderson), various classes of Vajrayana literature developed as a result of royal courts sponsoring both Buddhism and [Saivism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaivism). Sanderson has argued that Buddhist tantras can be shown to have borrowed practices, terms, rituals and more form Shaiva tantras. He argues that Buddhist texts even directly copied various Shaiva tantras, especially the Bhairava Vidyapitha tantras.[^cite_note-478][^cite_note-479] Ronald M. Davidson meanwhile, argues that Sanderson's claims for direct influence from Shaiva *Vidyapitha* texts are problematic because "the chronology of the *Vidyapitha* tantras is by no means so well established"[^cite_note-480] and that the Shaiva tradition also appropriated non-Hindu deities, texts and traditions. Thus while "there can be no question that the Buddhist tantras were heavily influenced by [Kapalika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kapalika) and other Saiva movements" argues Davidson, "the influence was apparently mutual".[^cite_note-481]

Already during this later era, Buddhism was losing state support in other regions of India, including the lands of the [Karkotas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karkota_Empire), the [Pratiharas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gurjara-Pratihara_dynasty), the [Rashtrakutas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashtrakuta_dynasty), the [Pandyas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pandya_dynasty) and the [Pallavas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pallava_dynasty). This loss of support in favor of Hindu faiths like [Vaishnavism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaishnavism) and [Shaivism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaivism), is the beginning of the long and complex period of the [Decline of Buddhism in the Indian subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decline_of_Buddhism_in_the_Indian_subcontinent).[^cite_note-482] The [Islamic invasions and conquest of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquests_in_the_Indian_subcontinent) (10th to 12th century), further damaged and destroyed many Buddhist institutions, leading to its eventual near disappearance from India by the 1200s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECollins2000184–185-483]

### Spread to East and Southeast Asia



[Angkor Thom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angkor_Thom) build by [Khmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khmer_Empire) King [Jayavarman VII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jayavarman_VII) (c. 1120–1218)

The [Silk Road transmission of Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silk_Road_transmission_of_Buddhism) to China is most commonly thought to have started in the late 2nd or the 1st century CE, though the literary sources are all open to question.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZürcher197222–27-484][^cite_note-Hill-486] The first documented translation efforts by foreign [Buddhist monks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_monk) in China were in the 2nd century CE, probably as a consequence of the expansion of the [Kushan Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kushan_Empire) into the Chinese territory of the [Tarim Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarim_Basin).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZürcher197223-487]

The first documented Buddhist texts translated into Chinese are those of the Parthian [An Shigao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/An_Shigao) (148–180 CE).[^cite_note-488] The first known [Mahāyāna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahayana) scriptural texts are translations into Chinese by the Kushan monk [Lokakṣema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lokaksema_\(Buddhist_monk\)) in [Luoyang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luoyang), between 178 and 189 CE.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams200830-489] From China, Buddhism was introduced into its neighbours [Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korea) (4th century), Japan (6th–7th centuries), and Vietnam (c. 1st–2nd centuries).[^cite_note-Dykstra,_Yoshiko_Kurata_2001_p._100-490][^cite_note-491]

During the Chinese [Tang dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tang_dynasty) (618–907), [Chinese Esoteric Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Esoteric_Buddhism) was introduced from India and [Chan Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chan_Buddhism) (Zen) became a major religion.[^cite_note-492][^cite_note-493] Chan continued to grow in the [Song dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Song_dynasty) (960–1279) and it was during this era that it strongly influenced Korean Buddhism and Japanese Buddhism.[^cite_note-494] [Pure Land Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pure_Land_Buddhism) also became popular during this period and was often practised together with Chan.[^cite_note-495] It was also during the Song that the entire [Chinese canon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Buddhist_canon) was printed using over 130,000 wooden printing blocks.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2013223-496]

During the Indian period of Esoteric Buddhism (from the 8th century onwards), Buddhism spread from India to Tibet and [Mongolia](19271.md). Johannes Bronkhorst states that the esoteric form was attractive because it allowed both a secluded monastic community as well as the social rites and rituals important to laypersons and to kings for the maintenance of a political state during succession and wars to resist invasion.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBronkhorst2011242–246-497] During the Middle Ages, Buddhism slowly declined in India,[^cite_note-498] while it vanished from Persia and Central Asia as Islam became the state religion.[^cite_note-larsfogelin6-499][^cite_note-500]

The [Theravada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theravada) school arrived in Sri Lanka sometime in the 3rd century BCE. Sri Lanka became a base for its later spread to [Southeast Asia](28741.md) after the 5th century CE ([Myanmar](19457.md), Malaysia, Indonesia, Thailand, Cambodia and coastal Vietnam).[^cite_note-501][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESkilling1997-502] [Theravada Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theravada) was the dominant religion in [Burma](19457.md) during the Mon [Hanthawaddy kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanthawaddy_kingdom) (1287–1552).[^cite_note-503] It also became dominant in the [Khmer Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khmer_Empire) during the 13th and 14th centuries and in the Thai [Sukhothai Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukhothai_Kingdom) during the reign of [Ram Khamhaeng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ram_Khamhaeng) (1237/1247–1298).[^cite_note-504][^cite_note-505]

## Schools and traditions

Main articles: [Schools of Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schools_of_Buddhism) and [Timeline of Buddhism § Common Era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Buddhism#Common_Era)

Buddhists generally classify themselves as either [Theravāda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theravada) or [Mahāyāna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahayana).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeown199612-506] This classification is also used by some scholars[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith2006-507] and is the one ordinarily used in the English language.[^cite_note-508] An alternative scheme used by some scholars divides Buddhism into the following three traditions or geographical or cultural areas: Theravāda (or "Southern Buddhism", "South Asian Buddhism"), [East Asian Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Asian_Buddhism) (or just "Eastern Buddhism") and [Indo-Tibetan Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibetan_Buddhism) (or "Northern Buddhism").[^cite_note-alternative_scheme-509]



Buddhists of various traditions, Yeunten Ling Tibetan Institute

The Theravada tradition traces its origins as the oldest tradition holding the Pali Canon as the only authority. The Mahayana tradition reveres the Canon but also derivative literature that developed in the 1st millennium CE; its roots are traceable to the 1st century BCE. The Vajrayana tradition is closer to the Mahayana, includes Tantra, and as the younger of the three is traceable to the 1st millennium CE.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGethin19981–2,_49–58,_253–271-510][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams19891–25-511]

Some scholars use other schemes, such as the multi-dimensional classification in the *Encyclopedia of Religion*.[^cite_note-512] Buddhists themselves have a variety of other schemes. [Hinayana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinayana) (literally "lesser or inferior vehicle") is sometimes used by Mahāyāna followers to name the family of early philosophical schools and traditions from which contemporary Theravāda emerged, but as the Hinayana term is considered derogatory, a variety of other terms are used instead, including: [Śrāvakayāna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shravakayana), Nikaya Buddhism, early Buddhist schools, sectarian Buddhism and conservative Buddhism.[^cite_note-kwmorganp410-513][^cite_note-514]

Not all traditions of Buddhism share the same philosophical outlook or treat the same concepts as central. Each tradition, however, does have its own core concepts, and some comparisons can be drawn between them:[^cite_note-515][^cite_note-516]

- Both Theravāda and Mahāyāna accept and revere [the Buddha Sakyamuni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gautama_Buddha) as the founder, Mahāyāna also reveres numerous other Buddhas, such as [Amitabha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amit%C4%81bha) or [Vairocana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vairochana) as well as many other bodhisattvas not revered in Theravāda.

- Both accept the [Middle Way](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Way), [Dependent origination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prat%C4%ABtyasamutp%C4%81da), the [Four Noble Truths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Noble_Truths), the [Noble Eightfold Path](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noble_Eightfold_Path), the [Three Jewels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refuge_\(Buddhism\)), the [Three marks of existence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_marks_of_existence) and the *[Bodhipakṣadharmas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bodhipakkhiy%C4%81dhamm%C4%81)* (aids to awakening).

- Mahāyāna focuses mainly on the [bodhisattva path](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bodhisattva) to Buddhahood which it sees as universal and to be practiced by all persons, while Theravāda does not focus on teaching this path and teaches the attainment of [arhatship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arhat) as a worthy goal to strive towards. The bodhisattva path is not denied in Theravāda, it is generally seen as a long and difficult path suitable for only a few.

  [^cite_note-517]

  &#x20;Thus the Bodhisattva path is normative in Mahāyāna, while it is an optional path for a heroic few in Theravāda.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeownPrebish2013"Bodhisattva,_Career_in_the_Theravada"-518]

- Mahāyāna sees the arhat's nirvana as being imperfect and inferior or preliminary to full Buddhahood. It sees arhatship as selfish, since bodhisattvas vow to save all beings while arhats save only themselves.

  [^cite_note-519]

  &#x20;Theravāda meanwhile does not accept that the arhat's nirvana is an inferior or preliminary attainment, nor that it is a selfish deed to attain arhatship since not only are arhats described as compassionate but they have destroyed the root of greed, the sense of "I am".

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeownPrebish2013"Bodhisattva,_Career_in_the_Theravada"-518]

- Mahāyāna accepts the authority of the many Mahāyāna sutras along with the other Nikaya texts like the Agamas and the Pali Canon (though it sees Mahāyāna texts as primary), while Theravāda does not accept that the Mahāyāna sutras are *[buddhavacana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhavacana)* (word of the Buddha) at all.

  [^cite_note-520]

## Monasteries and temples

Main article: [Buddhist architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_architecture)

Various types of Buddhist buildings













Buddhist institutions are often housed and centred around [monasteries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monasteries) (Sanskrit: *[viharas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vih%C4%81ra)*) and temples. Buddhist monastics originally followed a life of wandering, never staying in one place for long. During the three-month rainy season (*[vassa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vassa)*) they would gather together in one place for a period of intense practice and then depart again.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHirakawa199368-521][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDutt198853-522] Some of the earliest Buddhist monasteries were at groves (*vanas*) or woods (*araññas*), such as [Jetavana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jetavana) and [Sarnath's Deer Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarnath). There originally seems to have been two main types of monasteries, monastic settlements (*sangharamas*) were built and supported by donors, and woodland camps (*avasas*) were set up by monks. Whatever structures were built in these locales were made out of wood and were sometimes temporary structures built for the rainy season.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHirakawa199334-523][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDutt198855-524] Over time, the wandering community slowly adopted more settled [cenobitic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cenobitic_monasticism) forms of monasticism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDutt198857–59-525]

There are many different forms of Buddhist structures. Classic Indian Buddhist institutions mainly made use of the following structures: monasteries, rock-hewn cave complexes (such as the [Ajanta Caves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ajanta_Caves)), [stupas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stupa) (funerary mounds which contained relics), and temples such as the [Mahabodhi Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahabodhi_Temple).[^cite_note-526] In Southeast Asia, the most widespread institutions are centred on [wats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wat). East Asian Buddhist institutions also use various structures including monastic halls, temples, lecture halls, bell towers and [pagodas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pagoda). In [Japanese Buddhist temples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_temples_in_Japan), these different structures are usually grouped together in an area termed the [garan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shichid%C5%8D_garan). In Indo-Tibetan Buddhism, Buddhist institutions are generally housed in [gompas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gompa). They include monastic quarters, stupas and prayer halls with Buddha images. In the modern era, the Buddhist "meditation centre", which is mostly used by laypersons and often also staffed by them, has also become widespread.[^cite_note-527]

## In the modern era

Main articles: [Buddhism by country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_by_country) and [Buddhist modernism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_modernism)

Buddhist monk in Siberia in robes leaning on railing looking at temple

[Buryat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buryats) Buddhist monk in [Siberia](29639.md)

### Colonial era and after

Buddhism has faced various challenges and changes during the colonisation of Buddhist states by Christian countries and its persecution under modern states. Like other religions, the findings of modern science have challenged its basic premises. One response to some of these challenges has come to be called [Buddhist modernism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_modernism). Early Buddhist modernist figures such as the American convert [Henry Olcott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Steel_Olcott) (1832–1907) and [Anagarika Dharmapala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anagarika_Dharmapala) (1864–1933) reinterpreted and promoted Buddhism as a scientific and rational religion which they saw as compatible with modern science.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2013378-528]

[East Asian Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Asian_Buddhism) meanwhile suffered under various wars which ravaged China during the modern era, such as the [Taiping rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiping_Rebellion) and [World War II](32927.md) (which also affected [Korean Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Buddhism)). During the [Republican period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_China_\(1912%E2%80%931949\)) (1912–49), a new movement called [Humanistic Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humanistic_Buddhism) was developed by figures such as [Taixu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taixu) (1899–1947), and though Buddhist institutions were destroyed during the [Cultural Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_Revolution) (1966–76), there has been a revival of the religion in China after 1977.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2013409–410-529] [Japanese Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Japan) also went through a period of modernisation during the [Meiji period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meiji_period).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2013403-530] In [Central Asia](6742.md) meanwhile, the arrival of [Communist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism) repression to [Tibet](31516.md) (1966–1980) and [Mongolia](19271.md) (between 1924 and 1990) had a strong negative impact on Buddhist institutions, though the situation has improved somewhat since the 80s and 90s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2013414–417-531]

In Afghanistan and Pakistan, militants have destroyed some historic Buddhist monuments.[^cite_note-532][^cite_note-533]

### In the West

Main article: [Buddhism in the West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_the_West)



1893 [World Parliament of Religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Parliament_of_Religions) in Chicago, [Illinois](14849.md), United States



Interior of the Thai Buddhist wat in [Nukari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nukari), [Nurmijärvi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nurmij%C3%A4rvi), Finland

While there were some encounters of Western travellers or missionaries such as St. [Francis Xavier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_Xavier) and [Ippolito Desideri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ippolito_Desideri) with Buddhist cultures, it was not until the 19th century that Buddhism began to be studied by Western scholars. It was the work of pioneering scholars such as [Eugène Burnouf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eug%C3%A8ne_Burnouf), [Max Müller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Max_M%C3%BCller), [Hermann Oldenberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hermann_Oldenberg) and [Thomas William Rhys Davids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_William_Rhys_Davids) that paved the way for modern [Buddhist studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_studies) in the West. The English words such as Buddhism, "Boudhist", "Bauddhist" and Buddhist were coined in the early 19th-century in the West,[^cite_note-534] while in 1881, Rhys Davids founded the [Pali Text Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pali_Text_Society)—an influential Western resource of Buddhist literature in the Pali language and one of the earliest publisher of a journal on [Buddhist studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_studies).[^cite_note-535] It was also during the 19th century that Asian Buddhist immigrants (mainly from China and Japan) began to arrive in Western countries such as the United States and Canada, bringing with them their Buddhist religion. This period also saw the first Westerners to formally convert to Buddhism, such as [Helena Blavatsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helena_Blavatsky) and [Henry Steel Olcott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Steel_Olcott).[^cite_note-536] An important event in the introduction of Buddhism to the West was the 1893 [World Parliament of Religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Parliament_of_Religions), which for the first time saw well-publicised speeches by major Buddhist leaders alongside other religious leaders.

The 20th century saw a prolific growth of new Buddhist institutions in Western countries,[^cite_note-brit-537] including the [Buddhist Society, London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_Society,_London) (1924), [Das Buddhistische Haus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Das_Buddhistische_Haus) (1924) and [Datsan Gunzechoinei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Datsan_Gunzechoinei) in [St Petersburg](24320051.md). The publication and translations of Buddhist literature in Western languages thereafter accelerated. After the [second world war](32927.md), further immigration from Asia, globalisation, the [secularisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularisation) on Western culture as well a renewed interest in Buddhism among the 60s [counterculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counterculture) led to further growth in Buddhist institutions.[^cite_note-538] Influential figures on post-war [Western Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_the_West) include [Shunryu Suzuki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shunryu_Suzuki), [Jack Kerouac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jack_Kerouac), [Alan Watts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_Watts), [Thích Nhất Hạnh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Th%C3%ADch_Nh%E1%BA%A5t_H%E1%BA%A1nh), and the [14th Dalai Lama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/14th_Dalai_Lama). While Buddhist institutions have grown, some of the central premises of Buddhism such as the cycles of rebirth and [Four Noble Truths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Noble_Truths) have been problematic in the West.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKonik2009ix-539][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHayes2013172-540][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELamb2001258-541] In contrast, states Christopher Gowans, for "most ordinary \[Asian] Buddhists, today as well as in the past, their basic moral orientation is governed by belief in karma and rebirth".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGowans201418–23,_76–88-542] Most Asian Buddhist laypersons, states Kevin Trainor, have historically pursued Buddhist rituals and practices seeking better rebirth,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeown200960–63,_74–85,_185–187-543] not nirvana or freedom from rebirth.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFowler199965-544]

Buddha statue in 1896, Bamiyan

After statue destroyed by Islamist Taliban in 2001

[Buddhas of Bamiyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhas_of_Bamiyan), Afghanistan in 1896 (top) and after destruction in 2001 by the [Taliban](30635.md) Islamists.

[^cite_note-545]

Buddhism has spread across the world,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHenderson200242-546][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETamney199868-547] and Buddhist texts are increasingly translated into local languages. While [Buddhism in the West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_the_West) is often seen as exotic and progressive, in the East it is regarded as familiar and traditional. In countries such as Cambodia and [Bhutan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhutan), it is recognised as the [state religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_religion) and receives government support.

### Neo-Buddhism movements

Main articles: [Dalit Buddhist movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dalit_Buddhist_movement), [Navayana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navayana), and [Twenty-two vows of Ambedkar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twenty-two_vows_of_Ambedkar)

A number of modern movements in Buddhism emerged during the second half of the 20th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEParanjpe1998351-548][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPavāra2009xv–xviii-549] These [new forms of Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_modernism) are diverse and significantly depart from traditional beliefs and practices.[^cite_note-550]

In India, [B.R. Ambedkar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B.R._Ambedkar) launched the Navayana tradition—literally, "new vehicle". Ambedkar's Buddhism rejects the foundational doctrines and historic practices of traditional Theravada and Mahayana traditions, such as monk lifestyle after renunciation, karma, rebirth, samsara, meditation, nirvana, Four Noble Truths and others.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeownPrebish201325-551][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEQueen2013524–529-552][^cite_note-Skaria_2015-553] Ambedkar's Navayana Buddhism considers these as superstitions and re-interprets the original Buddha as someone who taught about [class struggle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Class_conflict) and social equality.[^cite_note-Zelliot-554][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeownPrebish201324–26-555] Ambedkar urged low caste Indian [Dalits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dalit) to convert to his Marxism-inspired[^cite_note-Skaria_2015-553] reinterpretation called the [Navayana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navayana) Buddhism, also known as Bhimayana Buddhism. Ambedkar's effort led to the expansion of Navayana Buddhism in India.[^cite_note-556][^cite_note-Zelliot-554]

The Thai King [Mongkut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongkut) (r. 1851–68), and his son [Chulalongkorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chulalongkorn) (r. 1868–1910), were responsible for modern reforms of [Thai Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Thailand).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2013385-557] Modern Buddhist movements include [Secular Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secular_Buddhism) in many countries, [Won Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Won_Buddhism) in Korea, the [Dhammakaya movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhammakaya_movement) in Thailand and several Japanese organisations, such as [Shinnyo-en](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shinnyo-en), [Risshō Kōsei Kai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rissh%C5%8D_K%C5%8Dsei_Kai) or [Soka Gakkai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soka_Gakkai).

Some of these movements have brought internal disputes and strife within regional Buddhist communities. For example, the Dhammakaya movement in Thailand teaches a "true self" doctrine, which traditional Theravada monks consider as heretically denying the fundamental *anatta* ([not-self](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatt%C4%81)) doctrine of Buddhism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams2008125–128-558][^cite_note-559][^cite_note-560]

### Sexual abuse and misconduct

Buddhism has not been immune from sexual abuse and misconduct scandals, with victims coming forward in various Buddhist schools such as [Zen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zen) and [Tibetan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibetan_Buddhism).[^cite_note-561][^cite_note-562][^cite_note-563] "There are huge cover ups in the Catholic church, but what has happened within Tibetan Buddhism is totally along the same lines," says Mary Finnigan, an author and journalist who has been chronicling such alleged abuses since the mid-80s.[^cite_note-564] One notably covered [case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sogyal_Rinpoche#Abuse_allegations) in media of various Western countries was that of [Sogyal Rinpoche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sogyal_Rinpoche) which began in 1994,[^cite_note-565] and ended with his retirement from his position as [Rigpa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rigpa_\(organization\))'s spiritual director in 2017.[^cite_note-566]

### Classification

There is consensus among [religious studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_studies) scholars that Buddhism is a religion.[^cite_note-Goble_2019_p.-567] However, Buddhism has posed problems to Western scholars of religion who define religion based solely on a "theistic conception".[^cite_note-Herbrechtsmeier_1993_p._1-568][^cite_note-Lion's_Roar-569] Further, some [Western Buddhists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Buddhism) and commentators like [Alan Watts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_Watts) maintain that Buddhism does not constitute a religion but rather a philosophy, a [psychotherapy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychotherapy), or a [way of life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Practical_philosophy).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEvan_der_Velde201422-570][^cite_note-Aich_2013_p._165-571][^cite_note-Lion's_Roar-569] This conception is rooted in 19th century [orientalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orientalism) writers, such as [theosophist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theosophy) [Henry Steel Olcott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Steel_Olcott), which reinterpreted Buddhism in a Protestant lens and viewed contemporary Buddhism in Asia as representing a debased religious form of what was originally non-religious and rational.[^cite_note-572] Some Buddhist teachers and commentators, such as *Dharmavidya* David Brazier, have criticised the persistence of this view.[^cite_note-Brazier_Brazier_2015_h916-573][^cite_note-574] Among Buddhists in Sri Lanka, Buddhism is parallel to Hinduism, Islam, and Christianity as an *āgama*,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESouthwold1978363-575] literally "scripture" or "teaching".[^cite_note-576]

## Cultural influence

Main article: [Culture of Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Buddhism)



Lhasa's [Potala Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potala_Palace), today a [UNESCO](21786641.md) [World Heritage Site](44940.md), pictured in 2019



India's [Mahabodhi Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahabodhi_Temple), built under the [Gupta Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gupta_Empire), 6th century CE

Buddhism has had a profound influence on various cultures, especially in Asia. [Buddhist philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_philosophy), [Buddhist art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_art), [Buddhist architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_architecture), [Buddhist cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_cuisine) and [Buddhist festivals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_festivals) continue to be influential elements of the modern [Culture of Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Asia), especially in [East Asia](19605700.md) and the [Sinosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Asian_cultural_sphere) as well as in [Southeast Asia](28741.md) and the [Indosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indosphere). According to Litian Fang, Buddhism has "permeated a wide range of fields, such as politics, ethics, philosophy, literature, art and customs", in these Asian regions.[^cite_note-577] Buddhist teachings influenced the development of modern Hinduism as well as other [Asian religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Asia) like [Taoism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taoism) and [Confucianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confucianism).
