Buddhist philosophers like [Dignaga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dign%C4%81ga) and [Dharmakirti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharmakirti) were very influential in the development of [Indian logic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_logic) and [epistemology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pramana).[^cite_note-578] Buddhist educational institutions like [Nalanda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nalanda) and [Vikramashila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vikramashila) preserved various disciplines of classical Indian knowledge such as grammar, astronomy/astrology and medicine and taught foreign students from Asia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDutt1988332–333-579]

In the Western world, Buddhism has had a strong influence on modern [New Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Age) spirituality and other alternative spiritualities. This began with its influence on 20th century [Theosophists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theosophists) such as [Helena Blavatsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helena_Blavatsky), which were some of the first Westerners to take Buddhism seriously as a spiritual tradition.[^cite_note-580] More recently, Buddhist meditation practices have influenced the development of modern [psychology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychology), particularly the practice of [Mindfulness-based stress reduction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mindfulness-based_stress_reduction) (MBSR) and other similar [mindfulness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mindfulness) based modalities.[^cite_note-581][^cite_note-582] The influence of [Buddhism on psychology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_and_psychology) can also be seen in certain forms of modern [psychoanalysis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychoanalysis).[^cite_note-583][^cite_note-584]

## Demographics

See also: [Buddhism by country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_by_country)



Global percentage of adherents by religion. Buddhists comprise about 4.1% of the world's population.

[^cite_note-Pew_20250609-585]

China40.5/

Percentage of [Buddhists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhists) by country in 2010, according to the [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center)

Buddhism is practised by an estimated 324 million in 2020, representing 4.1% of the world's total population.[^cite_note-populationchange-586] Buddhists are the only major religious group that declined in size between 2010 and 2020, according to the Pew Research Center, dropping from about 343 million to 324 million people; a decrease of roughly 5%.[^cite_note-Tong-587][^cite_note-pewresearch_2026-03-11-588] This decline is attributed to demographic factors such as an aging population and low fertility rates, as well as religious disaffiliation, with many individuals raised as Buddhists no longer identifying with the religion in adulthood.[^cite_note-Tong-587][^cite_note-pewresearch_2026-03-11-588]

[Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Thailand) is the country with the largest population of [Buddhists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhists),[^cite_note-Hackett-2025-589] approximately 64 million or 94.4% of its total population.[^cite_note-Hackett-2025-589] China is the second-largest country with the largest population of Buddhists, approximately 42 million or 4% of its total population.[^cite_note-590][^cite_note-591][^cite_note-religion2023-592] They are mostly followers of [Chinese schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Buddhism) of [Mahayana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahayana), making it the largest branch of Buddhism. Mahayana, also practised in broader [East Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_East_Asia), is followed by over half of world Buddhists.[^cite_note-Pew_2012a-593] [Theravada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theravada) is the second-largest branch and is mostly followed in [Mainland Southeast Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainland_Southeast_Asia) and Sri Lanka with around 36% population as of 2010.[^cite_note-594]

Buddhism is the dominant religion in [Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Thailand), [Cambodia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Cambodia), [Myanmar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Myanmar), [Sri Lanka](1164480.md), [Bhutan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Bhutan), [Laos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Laos), [Mongolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Mongolia), [Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Japan),[^cite_note-595][^cite_note-596] [Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Singapore)[^cite_note-597] and [Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Vietnam).[^cite_note-598] Large Buddhist populations live in [mainland China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Buddhism), [Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Taiwan), [North Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Korea), [Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Nepal) and [South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Korea).[^cite_note-The_Pew_Forum-599] The Indian state of [Maharashtra](20629.md) accounts for 77% of all Buddhists in India.[^cite_note-600] In Russia, Buddhists form majority in [Tuva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuva) (52%) and [Kalmykia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalmykia) (53%). [Buryatia](741441.md) (20%) and [Zabaykalsky Krai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zabaykalsky_Krai) (15%) also have significant Buddhist populations.[^cite_note-601]

Buddhism is the only major religion to have declined massively in the last decade,[^cite_note-Tong-587] particularly due to growing irreligion in East Asia.[^cite_note-populationchange-586] Buddhism has seen growth only in parts of India where marginalized [Dalits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dalits) Hindus have converted to Buddhism,[^cite_note-financialexpress-dalits-602][^cite_note-thequint.com-603] and they are called [Navayana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navayana).[^cite_note-financialexpress-dalits-602][^cite_note-thequint.com-603] In New Zealand, about 25–35% of the total Buddhists are converts to Buddhism.[^cite_note-604][^cite_note-605] Buddhism has also spread to the [Nordic countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_countries); for example, the Burmese Buddhists founded in the city of [Kuopio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuopio) in [North Savo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Savo) the first Buddhist monastery of Finland, named the Buddha Dhamma Ramsi monastery.[^cite_note-606]

[^cite_note-pain-12]: &#x20;As opposite to *sukha*, "pleasure", it is better translated as "pain".<sup>[\[88\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmmanuel201326–31-103)</sup>

[^cite_note-48]: &#x20;Buddhist texts such as the [Jataka tales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jataka_tales) of the Theravada Buddhist tradition, and early biographies such as the *[Buddhacarita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhacarita)*, the [Lokottaravādin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lokottarav%C4%81da) *[Mahāvastu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mah%C4%81vastu)*, the [Sarvāstivādin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarvastivada) *[Lalitavistara Sūtra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lalitavistara_S%C5%ABtra)*, give different accounts about the life of the Buddha; many include stories of his many rebirths, and some add significant embellishments.<sup>[\[38\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTESwearer2004177-41)</sup><sup>[\[39\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGethin199815–24-42)</sup> Keown and Prebish state, "In the past, modern scholars have generally accepted 486 or 483 BCE for this \[Buddha's death], but the consensus is now that they rest on evidence which is too flimsy.<sup>[\[40\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeownPrebish2010105–106-43)</sup> Scholars are hesitant to make unqualified claims about the historical facts of the Buddha's life. Most accept that he lived, taught and founded a monastic order, but do not consistently accept all of the details contained in his biographies."<sup>[\[41\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBuswell2004352-44)</sup><sup>[\[42\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTELopez199516-45)</sup><sup>[\[43\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTECarrithers198610-46)</sup><sup>[\[44\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEArmstrong2004xii-47)</sup>

[^cite_note-49]: &#x20;The exact identity of this ancient place is unclear. Please see [Gautama Buddha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gautama_Buddha) article for various sites identified.

[^cite_note-51]: &#x20;Bihar is derived from *Vihara*, which means monastery.<sup>[\[45\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich198849-50)</sup>

[^cite_note-64]: &#x20;The earliest Buddhist biographies of the Buddha mention these Vedic-era teachers. Outside of these early Buddhist texts, these names do not appear, which has led some scholars to raise doubts about the historicity of these claims.<sup>[\[54\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWynne20078–23-60)</sup><sup>[\[56\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBronkhorst201319–32-62)</sup> According to Alexander Wynne, the evidence suggests that Buddha studied under these Vedic-era teachers and they "almost certainly" taught him, but the details of his education are unclear.<sup>[\[54\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWynne20078–23-60)</sup><sup>[\[57\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHirakawa199322–26-63)</sup>

[^cite_note-Samsara-84]: &#x20;On samsara, rebirth and redeath:\
    \* Paul Williams: "All rebirth is due to karma and is impermanent. Short of attaining enlightenment, in each rebirth one is born and dies, to be reborn elsewhere in accordance with the completely impersonal causal nature of one's own karma. The endless cycle of birth, rebirth, and redeath, is samsara."<sup>[\[71\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams200274–75-78)</sup>\
    \* Buswell and Lopez on "rebirth": "An English term that does not have an exact correlate in Buddhist languages, rendered instead by a range of technical terms, such as the Sanskrit *Punarjanman* (lit. 'birth again') and *Punabhavan* (lit. 're-becoming'), and, less commonly, the related [PUNARMRTYU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redeath) (lit. 'redeath')."<sup>[\[73\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBuswellLopez2003708-80)</sup>\
    \
    See also Perry Schmidt-Leukel (2006) pp. 32–34,<sup>[\[74\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchmidt-Leukel200632–34-81)</sup> John J. Makransky (1997) p. 27.<sup>[\[75\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMakransky199727-82)</sup> for the use of the term "redeath". The term *Agatigati* or *Agati gati* (plus a few other terms) is generally translated as 'rebirth, redeath'; see any Pali-English dictionary; e.g. pp. 94–95 of Rhys Davids & William Stede, where they list five Sutta examples with rebirth and re-death sense.<sup>[\[76\]](#cite_note-83)</sup>

[^cite_note-Moksha-101]: &#x20;Graham Harvey: "Siddhartha Gautama found an end to rebirth in this world of suffering. His teachings, known as the dharma in Buddhism, can be summarized in the Four Noble truths."<sup>[\[79\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2016-88)</sup> Geoffrey Samuel (2008): "The Four Noble Truths \[...] describe the knowledge needed to set out on the path to liberation from rebirth."<sup>[\[80\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTESamuel2008136-89)</sup> See also:<sup>[\[81\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTESpiro198242-90)</sup><sup>[\[82\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVetter1988xxi,_xxxi–xxxii-91)</sup><sup>[\[83\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMakransky199727–28-92)</sup><sup>[\[71\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams200274–75-78)</sup><sup>[\[72\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTELopez2009147-79)</sup><sup>[\[79\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2016-88)</sup><sup>[\[84\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKingsland2016286-93)</sup><sup>[\[web 2\]](#cite_note-EB-DL_Four_Truths-94)</sup><sup>[\[web 3\]](#cite_note-95)</sup>\
    \
    The Theravada tradition holds that insight into these four truths is liberating in itself.<sup>[\[85\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTECarter19873179-96)</sup> This is reflected in the Pali Canon.<sup>[\[86\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnderson2013-97)</sup> According to Donald Lopez, "The Buddha stated in his first sermon that when he gained absolute and intuitive knowledge of the four truths, he achieved complete enlightenment and freedom from future rebirth."<sup>[\[web 2\]](#cite_note-EB-DL_Four_Truths-94)</sup>\
    \
    The *[Maha-parinibbana Sutta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maha-parinibbana_Sutta)* also refers to this liberation.<sup>[\[web 4\]](#cite_note-98)</sup> Carol Anderson: "The second passage where the four truths appear in the *Vinaya-pitaka* is also found in the *Mahaparinibbana-sutta* (D II 90–91). Here, the Buddha explains that it is by not understanding the four truths that rebirth continues."<sup>[\[87\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnderson2013162_with_note_38,_for_context_see_pp._1–3-99)</sup>\
    \
    On the meaning of moksha as liberation from rebirth, see Patrick Olivelle in the *Encyclopædia Britannica*.<sup>[\[web 5\]](#cite_note-Brittanica-100)</sup>

[^cite_note-realms2-123]: &#x20;Earlier Buddhist texts refer to five realms rather than six realms; when described as five realms, the god realm and demi-god realm constitute a single realm.<sup>[\[107\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBuswell2004711–712-122)</sup>

[^cite_note-133]: &#x20;This merit gaining may be on the behalf of one's family members.<sup>[\[114\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBuswellLopez2003708–709-130)</sup><sup>[\[115\]](#cite_note-Neufeldt1986p123-131)</sup><sup>[\[116\]](#cite_note-SwatosKivisto1998p66-132)</sup>

[^cite_note-152]: &#x20;Another variant, which may be condensed to the eightfold or tenfold path, starts with a *Tathagatha* entering this world. A layman hears his teachings, decides to leave the life of a householder, starts living according to the moral precepts, guards his sense-doors, practises mindfulness and the four jhanas, gains the three knowledges, understands the Four Noble Truths and destroys the [taints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asava), and perceives that he is liberated.<sup>[\[134\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBucknell1984-151)</sup>

[^cite_note-164]: &#x20;The early Mahayana Buddhism texts link their discussion of "emptiness" (*shunyata*) to *Anatta* and *Nirvana*. They do so, states Mun-Keat Choong, in three ways: first, in the common sense of a monk's meditative state of emptiness; second, with the main sense of *anatta* or 'everything in the world is empty of self'; third, with the ultimate sense of *nirvana* or realisation of emptiness and thus an end to rebirth cycles of suffering.<sup>[\[145\]](#cite_note-Choong1999p85-163)</sup>

[^cite_note-167]: &#x20;Some scholars such as Cousins and Sangharakshita translate *apranaihita* as "aimlessness or directionless-ness".<sup>[\[147\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams2005b56,_note_23-166)</sup>

[^cite_note-175]: &#x20;These descriptions of nirvana in Buddhist texts, states Peter Harvey, are contested by scholars because nirvana in Buddhism is ultimately described as a state of "stopped consciousness (blown out), but one that is not non-existent", and "it seems impossible to imagine what awareness devoid of any object would be like".<sup>[\[153\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201375–76-173)</sup><sup>[\[154\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGethin199874–84-174)</sup>

[^cite_note-180]: &#x20;Better rebirth, not nirvana, has been the primary focus of a vast majority of lay Buddhists. This they attempt through merit accumulation and good *kamma*.<sup>[\[157\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGowans2004169-178)</sup><sup>[\[158\]](#cite_note-Merv_Fowler_1999_65-179)</sup>

[^cite_note-218]: &#x20;Wayman and Wayman have disagreed with this view, and they state that the *Tathagatagarbha* is neither self nor sentient being, nor soul, nor personality.<sup>[\[195\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams2008107-217)</sup>

[^cite_note-306]: &#x20;Many ancient [Upanishads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upanishad) of Hinduism describe [yoga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoga) and meditation as a means to liberation.<sup>[\[279\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTECollins2000199-303)</sup><sup>[\[280\]](#cite_note-304)</sup><sup>[\[281\]](#cite_note-305)</sup>

[^cite_note-385]: &#x20;While some interpretations state that Buddhism may have originated as a social reform, other scholars state that it is incorrect and anachronistic to regard the Buddha as a social reformer.<sup>[\[358\]](#cite_note-QueenKing1996p17-383)</sup> Buddha's concern was "to reform individuals, help them to leave society forever, not to reform the world... he never preached against social inequality". [Richard Gombrich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Gombrich), quoted by Christopher Queen.<sup>[\[358\]](#cite_note-QueenKing1996p17-383)</sup><sup>[\[359\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich198830–31-384)</sup>

Norman,<sup>[\[384\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorman1992-412)</sup> the textual studies by Richard Gombrich,<sup>[\[385\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich1997-413)</sup> and the research on early meditation methods by Johannes Bronkhorst.<sup>[\[301\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBronkhorst1993-326)</sup>

[^cite_note-417]: &#x20;According to A.K. Warder, in his 1970 publication "Indian Buddhism", from the oldest extant texts a common kernel can be drawn out.<sup>[\[381\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWarder2000-408)</sup> According to Warder, c.q. his publisher: "This kernel of doctrine is presumably common Buddhism of the period before the great schisms of the fourth and third centuries BC. It may be substantially the Buddhism of the Buddha himself, although this cannot be proved: at any rate it is a Buddhism presupposed by the schools as existing about a hundred years after the parinirvana of the Buddha, and there is no evidence to suggest that it was formulated by anyone else than the Buddha and his immediate followers."<sup>[\[387\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWarder2000inside_flap-416)</sup>

[^cite_note-419]: &#x20;Richard Gombrich: "I have the greatest difficulty in accepting that the main edifice is not the work of a single genius. By "the main edifice" I mean the collections of the main body of sermons, the four Nikāyas, and of the main body of monastic rules."<sup>[\[385\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich1997-413)</sup>

[^cite_note-421]: &#x20;Ronald Davidson: "While most scholars agree that there was a rough body of sacred literature (disputed) \[*[sic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sic)*] that a relatively early community (disputed) \[*[sic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sic)*] maintained and transmitted, we have little confidence that much, if any, of surviving Buddhist scripture is actually the word of the historic Buddha."<sup>[\[389\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavidson2003147-420)</sup>

[^cite_note-Jong-423]: &#x20;J.W. De Jong: "It would be hypocritical to assert that nothing can be said about the doctrine of earliest Buddhism \[...] the basic ideas of Buddhism found in the canonical writings could very well have been proclaimed by him \[the Buddha], transmitted and developed by his disciples and, finally, codified in fixed formulas."<sup>[\[390\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJong199325-422)</sup>

[^cite_note-424]: &#x20;Bronkhorst: "This position is to be preferred to (ii) for purely methodological reasons: only those who seek may find, even if no success is guaranteed."<sup>[\[386\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBronkhorst1993vii-415)</sup>

[^cite_note-426]: &#x20;Lopez: "The original teachings of the historical Buddha are extremely difficult, if not impossible, to recover or reconstruct."<sup>[\[391\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTELopez19954-425)</sup>

[^cite_note-China_Buswell_2004-463]: &#x20;"The most important evidence – in fact the only evidence – for situating the emergence of the Mahayana around the beginning of the common era was not Indian evidence at all, but came from China. Already by the last quarter of the 2nd century CE, there was a small, seemingly idiosyncratic collection of substantial Mahayana sutras translated into what Erik Zürcher calls 'broken Chinese' by an Indoscythian, whose Indian name has been reconstructed as Lokaksema."<sup>[\[427\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBuswell2004492-462)</sup>

[^cite_note-South-466]: &#x20;"The south (of India) was then vigorously creative in producing Mahayana Sutras" Warder.<sup>[\[429\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWarder2000335-465)</sup>

[^cite_note-36]: &#x20;*Beyond Enlightenment: Buddhism, Religion, Modernity* by Richard Cohen. Routledge 1999. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [0-415-54444-0](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/0-415-54444-0). p. 33. "Donors adopted Sakyamuni Buddha's family name to assert their legitimacy as his heirs, both institutionally and ideologically. To take the name of Sakya was to define oneself by one's affiliation with the buddha, somewhat like calling oneself a Buddhist today.

[^cite_note-37]: &#x20;*Sakya or Buddhist Origins* by Caroline Rhys Davids (London: Kegan Paul, Trench, Trubner, 1931) p. 1. "Put away the word "Buddhism" and think of your subject as "Sakya." This will at once place you for your perspective at a true point. You are now concerned to learn less about 'Buddha' and 'Buddhism,' and more about him whom India has ever known as Sakya-muni, and about his men who, as their records admit, were spoken of as the Sakya-sons, or men of the Sakyas."

[^cite_note-38]: &#x20;Lopez, Donald S. (1995). *Curators of the Buddha*, University of Chicago Press. p. 7

[^cite_note-39]: &#x20;*Beyond Enlightenment: Buddhism, Religion, Modernity* by Richard Cohen. Routledge 1999. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [0-415-54444-0](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/0-415-54444-0). p. 33. Bauddha is "a secondary derivative of buddha, in which the vowel's lengthening indicates connection or relation. Things that are bauddha pertain to the buddha, just as things Saiva related to Siva and things Vaisnava belong to Visnu. ... baudda can be both adjectival and nominal; it can be used for doctrines spoken by the buddha, objects enjoyed by him, texts attributed to him, as well as individuals, communities, and societies that offer him reverence or accept ideologies certified through his name. Strictly speaking, Sakya is preferable to bauddha since the latter is not attested at Ajanta. In fact, as a collective noun, bauddha is an outsider's term. The bauddha did not call themselves this in India, though they did sometimes use the word adjectivally (e.g., as a possessive, the buddha's)."

Although the term Four Noble Truths is well known in English, it is a misleading translation of the Pali term Chattari-ariya-saccani (Sanskrit: Chatvari-arya-satyani), because noble (Pali: ariya; Sanskrit: arya) refers not to the truths themselves but to those who recognize and understand them. A more accurate rendering, therefore, might be 'four truths for the \[spiritually] noble'

[^cite_note-5sourcesanatta-111]: &#x20;*\[a]* [Anatta](https://www.britannica.com/topic/anatta) [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20210122042635/https://www.britannica.com/topic/anatta) 22 January 2021 at the [Wayback Machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayback_Machine), *[Encyclopædia Britannica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Encyclop%C3%A6dia_Britannica)* (2013), Quote: "Anatta in Buddhism, the doctrine that there is in humans no permanent, underlying soul.     *\[b]* Steven Collins (1994), Religion and Practical Reason (Editors: Frank Reynolds, David Tracy), State Univ of New York Press, [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0-7914-2217-5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0-7914-2217-5), p. 64; "Central to Buddhist [soteriology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soteriology) is the doctrine of [not-self](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatt%C4%81) (Pali: anattā, Sanskrit: anātman, the opposed doctrine of ātman is central to Brahmanical thought).     *\[c]* John C. Plott et al. [^cite_note-572]: &#x20;[Southwold 1978](#CITEREFSouthwold1978), pp. 362–379: "From Olcott's catechism grew the tradition of Buddhist ambivalence (if not outright hostility) toward the concept of religion, but his catechism had a religious origin in Olcott's own liberal Protestant Christian background. He took his challenge to be one of purifying Buddhism by returning to the fundamental teaching of the founder as recorded in its authoritative scriptures. The teaching he found in these texts had much in common with the liberal Protestantism of the late nineteenth century. It was opposed to 'superstitious' practices, suspicious of miracles and the supernatural, and respectful of the canons of reason."[Stewart 2018](#CITEREFStewart2018): "The view that Buddhism is a 'philosophy' and 'not a religion' is a prime example of nineteenth-century Orientalist scholarship entering into the Western public consciousness that persists even now \[...] \[b]ut such a view is overly reductionist."[van der Velde 2014](#CITEREFvan_der_Velde2014), pp. 30–31: "What was practice in 19th and early 20th century Asia was often considered aberrational, a perversion of what was once a pure practice. The purity of this practice was supposedly lost once Buddhism changed into the religion it now was in Asia. The original dharma could be reconstructed if the teachings were liberated from the 'cultural' and 'local' Asian context \[...] In fact, our perception of Buddhism is still colored by these presuppositions."
